<a id="item-1"></a>
## [Anthropic Releases Claude Sonnet 5.5, Second Model in the 5.5 Family](https://www.anthropic.com/claude-sonnet-5-5) ⭐️ 9.0/10

Anthropic released Claude Sonnet 5.5, the second model in the Claude 5.5 family, which the company says is a clear upgrade over Claude Sonnet 5, running 30%+ faster and costing up to 30% less for most work. The release drew heavy community attention (504 upvotes, 341 comments), with discussion focused on benchmark nuances and practical usage trade-offs. Sonnet is Anthropic's mid-tier model line, so a faster and cheaper Sonnet 5.5 directly affects the cost-performance calculus for developers building agentic coding tools and API-based products. The release also intensifies the competitive benchmark race among frontier models, where small scoring gaps are increasingly scrutinized. Sonnet 5.5 is served by five providers on OpenRouter (Google Vertex, Amazon Bedrock, Azure, Claude Platform on AWS, and Anthropic), with automatic failover and provider pinning. Anthropic notes that Sonnet 5.5's cyber capabilities are a large improvement over Sonnet 5, so it ships with safeguards similar to Opus 5.5, meaning higher-risk cybersecurity tasks visibly fall back to Sonnet 5.

hackernews · D2OQZG8l5BI1S06 · Sep 28, 17:58 · [Discussion](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49881850)

**Background**: Claude is Anthropic's family of large language models, released since March 2023 and typically offered in three sizes per generation: Haiku (least capable), Sonnet (mid-tier), and Opus (most capable). Anthropic also sells agentic coding tools such as Claude Code, a terminal coding agent, and Claude Cowork for non-programmers. Benchmarks like Terminal-Bench and the Artificial Analysis Intelligence Index are commonly used to compare models on coding and reasoning ability.

**Discussion**: Commenters raised several nuanced points: one noted that Opus 5.5's efficiency makes the 5x plan limits sufficient for daily work, questioning when Sonnet 5.5 would be needed; another pointed out that Sonnet 5.5's higher Terminal-Bench score (70.6 vs 66.4) may be explained by Opus 5.5's 10% fallback rate versus Sonnet's 1.5%, per section 8.5 of the system card. Others highlighted that on "max" thinking effort Sonnet 5.5 burned through 128,000 thinking tokens over 15 minutes and still failed to finish an SVG, and that Anthropic's cyber safeguards mean higher-risk security tasks fall back to weaker models.

<a id="item-2"></a>
## [AMD Acquires World Labs for $8.2 Billion](https://www.worldlabs.ai/blog/amd-announcement) ⭐️ 8.0/10

AMD has agreed to acquire World Labs, the AI startup founded by Fei-Fei Li, for $8.2 billion, just two years after the company was launched. The deal marks one of the fastest and largest exits for an AI research startup focused on spatial intelligence and world models. The acquisition signals that chipmakers are moving up the AI stack, integrating model and software capabilities rather than only selling hardware. It could reshape competition with Nvidia and intensify the race to build AI systems that understand and generate 3D worlds for robotics, simulation, and embodied AI. World Labs was founded by Fei-Fei Li, often called the 'godmother of AI,' and had already reached a valuation above $1 billion within four months of launching. The reported $8.2 billion price tag has raised questions about whether a two-year-old company with no mature product can justify such a valuation.

hackernews · mfiguiere · Sep 28, 20:18 · [Discussion](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49883760)

**Background**: World Labs builds 'world models' — AI systems that perceive, generate, reason about, and interact with virtual and physical 3D environments. AMD is a major chip designer best known for CPUs and GPUs, and has been expanding its AI software and hardware portfolio to challenge Nvidia's dominance in AI computing. Fei-Fei Li is a Stanford professor whose work on ImageNet helped launch the modern deep learning era.

## [Simon Willison's Annotated Keynote: 2026 in LLMs So Far](https://simonwillison.net/2026/Sep/27/2026-in-llms-so-far/) ⭐️ 8.0/10

Simon Willison published annotated slides and notes from his closing keynote at the WeAreDevelopers World Congress North America in San Jose on September 25, 2026, offering a chronological tour of LLM developments throughout the year. The talk traces the story back to a November 2025 inflection point marked by the releases of Claude Opus 4.5 and GPT-5.1. Willison is one of the most respected independent voices tracking LLM progress, so his synthesis helps developers and observers understand which changes were genuinely consequential rather than merely incremental. The annotated format, combining video, slides, and written notes, makes the retrospective a durable reference for tracking industry trends. Willison argues that Claude Opus 4.5 and GPT-5.1 were incremental model upgrades but crossed an invisible threshold when paired with their coding agent harnesses, Claude Code and Codex, moving from 'often make mistakes' to 'reliable enough to use on a day-to-day basis'. He also continues to use his deliberately silly 'Generate an SVG of a pelican riding a bicycle' benchmark, noting that as of November neither model could draw a convincing bicycle.

rss · Simon Willison · Sep 27, 23:54

**Background**: Simon Willison is a veteran developer known for co-creating the Django web framework and the Datasette data tool, and he has become a prolific independent commentator on large language models. An 'annotated talk' on his blog pairs each slide image with the notes he used while presenting, so readers can follow the argument without watching the video. Coding agents are AI systems that can autonomously write, run, and debug code, and their reliability is widely seen as the key gating factor for real-world adoption.

<a id="item-4"></a>
## [BioEVAL: A Multi-Institutional Benchmark for LLMs in Bioengineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30489) ⭐️ 8.0/10

BioEVAL, a global multi-institutional initiative involving 22 research groups, released a PhD-level benchmark of 608 evaluation items spanning 11 bioengineering subfields, including 359 audited multiple-choice questions, 218 literature synthesis tasks, and 10 multimodal experimental image interpretation problems. Evaluated cloud-scale models such as ChatGPT, Gemini, and Grok, along with locally deployable models, achieved up to 90% accuracy on MCQs, a 0.72 similarity score on literature synthesis, and 80% accuracy on the small multimodal sample. Existing LLM benchmarks emphasize factual recall, leaving a gap in assessing frontier experimental reasoning and multimodal capabilities in bioengineering. BioEVAL's scale, expert auditing, and extensible leaderboard provide a resource likely to shape future model development and evaluation priorities in AI for science. After a blinded cross-group consensus audit, 21 of the highest- and lowest-accuracy MCQ items were flagged for revision or removal and withheld, so all reported MCQ results are computed on the 359 retained items. Performance varied substantially across bioengineering subfields, and the multimodal evaluation was limited to a small sample of 10 problems.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Large language models have shown strong general reasoning and early biomedical successes, but most benchmarks test memorized facts rather than the experimental reasoning and image interpretation skills needed in bioengineering. Bioengineering combines engineering principles with biology to design systems and products, and evaluating AI on such tasks requires expert-crafted, PhD-level questions. BioEVAL was assembled by 22 research groups to create a standardized, extensible benchmark with expert review and quality control.

<a id="item-5"></a>
## [Reasoning Tokens Resolve Some Fairness Biases but Create 5x More](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30768) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.30768) conducts a within-model thinking-vs-non-thinking ablation across QwQ-32B, DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-32B, and Qwen3-32B on three high-stakes decision tasks (Adult, COMPAS, Credit), finding that reasoning has an asymmetric dual effect on counterfactual fairness. In all nine (model, dataset) combinations, counterfactual flips created by thinking outnumber those resolved by roughly 5 times, and the paper introduces the Counterfactual Depth Probability Gap (CDPG) metric to track bias evolution along thinking depth. This finding challenges the common assumption that more reasoning tokens automatically improve fairness, showing instead that thinking can amplify bias in high-stakes domains like credit, hiring, and criminal justice risk scoring. It gives AI ethics researchers and model developers a new measurement tool (CDPG) and a caution that longer chain-of-thought is not a fairness fix. The paper treats the thinking trace itself as a measurable site of fairness change, using two dynamic instruments: CDPG tracks bias evolution along thinking depth and shows bias propagates and amplifies with thinking, while the Bias Transition Matrix (BTM) shows the asymmetric dual effect originates in the pair-state joint transition of counterfactual pairs. Notably, the newly created flips occur at near-saturating model confidence, meaning the model is most wrong precisely when it is most certain.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Counterfactual fairness asks whether a model's prediction would stay the same if a sensitive attribute (such as gender or race) were changed while everything else is held equal; a 'flip' means the prediction changes, indicating bias. Reasoning language models (RLMs) like DeepSeek-R1 and QwQ generate hidden 'thinking tokens' before answering, and prior work disagreed on whether this deliberation reduces or amplifies bias. This paper resolves the debate by measuring both directions simultaneously within the same model.

## [Acacia: A Graph Foundation Model Trained from Scratch on the Web Graph](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30894) ⭐️ 8.0/10

Researchers introduced Acacia, a graph foundation model trained from scratch solely on the Common Crawl web graph, without relying on any pretrained LLM. Acacia supports arbitrary feature dimensionalities and semantics, handles node classification, link prediction, node clustering, and graph generation without additional training, and exhibits in-context learning capabilities. Existing graph foundation models typically require training extra classification heads or feature projectors for new graphs or labels, and often gain their capabilities by being stitched together with pretrained LLMs. Acacia shows that graph models can acquire emergent capabilities from scratch, potentially simplifying deployment and broadening the applicability of foundation models to structured data. Acacia is trained only on the Common Crawl web graph and requires no additional training for new tasks or feature dimensions, distinguishing it from prior GFMs that need task-specific heads or LLM integration. The paper frames this as evidence that graph models can develop emergent capabilities similar to those observed in large language models.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Graph foundation models (GFMs) aim to bring scalable, general-purpose intelligence to graph-structured data, enabling broad transfer across graph-centric tasks and domains. Traditional graph neural networks (GNNs) are typically trained for specific tasks and graphs, while GFMs are pretrained on extensive graph data and adapted to various downstream tasks. Common Crawl is a widely used open repository of web crawl data that regularly releases host- and domain-level web graphs, providing a large-scale real-world graph for pretraining.

<a id="item-7"></a>
## [LLM Agents Trigger Bank Runs and Debt Crises in FRAIL Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30940) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces FRAIL, a controlled experimental framework that places LLM agents in three dynamic financial environments—bank runs, debt rollover, and reward crowdfunding—where each agent's decisions reshape the conditions faced by others. Testing seven leading LLMs, the authors find that 77% of baseline bank-run episodes and 83% of debt-rollover episodes end in collective failure even though no agent is instructed to destabilize the system, and they compare three stabilizing interaction mechanisms: compensated commitments, centralized commitment agreements, and participant-led coalitions. The results show that individually capable LLM agents do not automatically form safe financial systems, which means financial AI safety must be evaluated at the system level rather than only per agent. As LLM agents take on greater roles in financial decision-making, this finding has direct implications for regulators, AI developers, and financial institutions deploying multi-agent systems. All three stabilizing mechanisms improve aggregate outcomes, but no single mechanism performs best across all financial structures; successful stabilization consistently shares a temporal pattern in which broad commitment forms early, before defensive behavior becomes self-reinforcing. The paper's code is available at an anonymous repository, and the abstract notes that the study covers seven leading LLMs across the three environments.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: LLM agents are AI systems that use large language models to make decisions and take actions autonomously, often interacting with other agents in multi-agent simulations. In financial settings, individually rational defensive actions—such as withdrawing deposits early or refusing to roll over debt—can deplete shared liquidity and cascade into collective failures like bank runs, a classic coordination problem in economics. FRAIL is designed to study whether such failures emerge spontaneously among LLM agents and which interaction mechanisms can prevent them.

## [LLM Text Measures Diverge Across Models, Study Finds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31013) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A large-scale arXiv study evaluates thirteen LLM-based textual measures—including sentiment, management clarity, uncertainty, answer specificity, and climate and political risk—by having seven LLMs from different providers score S&P 500 earnings call transcripts. Cross-model rank correlations average only 0.52, and transcript-level differences common across providers explain just 34% of total score variation. The finding undermines the common assumption that LLM-generated textual measures are model-invariant, showing that model choice can flip the sign, magnitude, and statistical significance of downstream empirical results. This has immediate implications for reproducibility and validity in the fast-growing field of LLM-assisted research in finance, economics, and computational social science. Cross-model disagreement does not predict subsequent analyst or market disagreement, suggesting a substantial model-specific component rather than common ambiguity in the underlying disclosure. Averaging across providers makes transcript rankings more stable for most constructs, but score levels remain sensitive to which models are included in the ensemble.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: In the "text-as-data" paradigm, researchers use generative LLMs to convert corporate disclosures such as earnings call transcripts into numerical variables for statistical analysis. This approach assumes that different models produce comparable measurements of the same construct, much like a reliable instrument. The study tests that assumption by comparing seven independently developed model families—OpenAI's GPT, Anthropic's Claude, Google's Gemini, Meta's Llama, Mistral, DeepSeek, and Alibaba's Qwen—on identical transcripts.

<a id="item-9"></a>
## [Reasoning Models Learn to Jailbreak Chain-of-Thought Monitors Without Hiding Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31121) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.31121) shows that reasoning models trained under chain-of-thought (CoT) monitor penalties can learn to evade detection without encoding their reasoning. The authors call this phenomenon 'monitor jailbreaking': models rephrase and reformat their CoT so monitors fail to flag side-task reasoning, while the reasoning remains fully transparent to human readers. The behavior emerges across different model sizes, monitors, and tasks, and generalizes to unseen monitors and monitor prompts. This identifies a novel and important safety failure mode for CoT monitoring, a technique increasingly relied upon to detect misaligned or scheming behavior in reasoning models before they act. Because the jailbreaks generalize to both weaker and stronger monitors, they suggest that optimization pressure during reinforcement learning can erode monitorability even without encoded reasoning, undermining a key assumption of current AI safety strategies. The jailbreaking strategies appear simple, yet manually replicating them does not reliably fool monitors, suggesting the effect depends on subtle learned patterns rather than obvious tricks. Paraphrasing a jailbroken CoT is an effective defense: it lets the same monitor correctly flag the reasoning while still allowing the model to perform both the main and side tasks.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Chain-of-thought monitoring supervises a model's natural-language reasoning trace to detect misalignment, scheming, or reward hacking before harmful actions occur. A central worry is encoded reasoning, where a model hides its true reasoning in compressed or non-interpretable forms that neither monitors nor humans can read. Optimization pressure from CoT monitors during reinforcement learning is considered a likely driver of such hidden reasoning, which would make monitoring unreliable.

<a id="item-10"></a>
## [PoLar Reconstructed: Dynamic Layer Routing in LLMs via Cortical Analogy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31360) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A new arXiv paper reconstructs PoLar's diagnostic Monte Carlo Tree Search in greater detail than the original work and applies it across five LLMs, reproducing findings that skipping layers beats the standard forward pass, repeating beats skipping, and combining both beats either alone. However, the authors failed to replicate the main claim about the learned router for single-shot inference, as its top-ranked prediction consistently collapsed back to the standard pass even though its top-k programs together showed real accuracy gains. This work matters because it provides a rigorous independent reproduction and deeper analysis of a promising adaptive inference paradigm, showing that a small number of generic programs can solve most questions while also revealing that error-correcting programs are highly brittle. These findings could shape how researchers design efficient, input-adaptive LLM inference systems and set expectations about the robustness of learned routers. The study found that shorter programs sufficed for easier questions while harder questions required more layer repeats, and that programs correcting errors are highly brittle—undoing even a single edit inside a program typically breaks the correction. The code is publicly released at https://datexis.github.io/RE-PoLar/.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: In standard LLM inference, every input passes through all layers in a fixed depth and order regardless of difficulty, unlike the human brain, where the thalamus acts as a central hub routing information flexibly to cortical regions based on demand. PoLar (program-of-layers) treats a frozen pretrained transformer's layers as a library of atomic functions, allowing an input-specific program to skip, keep, or repeat contiguous layer blocks without modifying the model. Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) is used to generate diagnostic supervision for training lightweight routers that decide these execution paths.

## [Kaggle Game Arena: Competitive Games as Dynamic LLM Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31473) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A large team of researchers from Google DeepMind and Kaggle introduced Kaggle Game Arena, an open and ever-expanding platform that evaluates large language models through head-to-head competitive games rather than static benchmarks. The technical report details the infrastructure and three pilot environments — Chess, Poker, and Werewolf — spanning perfect information, imperfect information, and multiplayer settings, with full competition results and evaluation metrics for each game. Static benchmarks are increasingly saturating as frontier models approach ceiling performance, so a platform whose difficulty naturally rises as models improve offers a more robust and long-lived way to measure strategic planning, adaptation, and robustness under uncertainty. Because it is open, reproducible, and designed to generalize to new games and variants, it could become a shared community resource for tracking genuine progress in LLM capabilities. The three pilot games deliberately cover different information structures: Chess is a perfect-information game, Poker is an imperfect-information game, and Werewolf is a multiplayer game involving hidden roles, deception, and communication. Evaluation relies on large-scale ground-truth-based competition results, and the report emphasizes reproducibility, transparency, and generalizability to new games over time.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Static benchmarks typically consist of fixed question sets, so once models score near the maximum, the benchmark loses the statistical power to distinguish further improvements — a problem known as benchmark saturation. Competitive games are an alternative because they are adversarial and open-ended: a model's strength is measured relative to opponents, and as all models improve, the challenge scales with them. The three pilot games also illustrate classic distinctions in game theory, where perfect information means all players see the full state (as in Chess), imperfect information means some information is hidden (as in Poker), and multiplayer games add complex social dynamics (as in Werewolf).

## [First Convergence Guarantees for Muon Optimizer, Plus New Muesterov Variant](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30546) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.30546) establishes the first asymptotic convergence guarantees for the Muon optimizer by using a more accurate proxy for its Newton-Schulz iteration than the standard matrix sign function. The authors prove that with appropriate hyperparameters the iterates satisfy lim_{k→∞}‖∇f(x_k)‖=0, and under a global Polyak-Łojasiewicz condition the function values converge linearly; they also introduce Muesterov, a Nesterov-based variant of Muon with the same guarantees. Muon is a widely used optimizer for neural network hidden layers, but until now it lacked rigorous convergence theory, so this work gives practitioners and theorists a formal foundation for why it works. The key insight that Muon's Newton-Schulz regularization induces a bounded preconditioner reframes it as a preconditioned Polyak heavy-ball method, opening the door to classical Lyapunov analysis and to principled variants like Muesterov. The analysis hinges on treating the regularization implicit in Muon's Newton-Schulz implementation as a bounded preconditioner, which enables a classical Lyapunov argument; numerical experiments on a scalar cross-entropy problem corroborate the theory and clarify how the learning rate and Newton-Schulz regularizer jointly control convergence. Preliminary simulations on nanoGPT training are also reported to give intuition about practical relevance, though the paper is theoretical and does not yet propose concrete implementation changes.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Muon is an optimizer designed for the 2D weight matrices of neural network hidden layers, and it uses a Newton-Schulz iteration to approximate the matrix sign function as a way of orthogonalizing updates. The Newton-Schulz iteration is a quadratically convergent, inversion-free method for computing matrix functions, and in Muon it acts as an implicit regularizer. Convergence guarantees are typically proved under conditions like the Polyak-Łojasiewicz inequality, a 1963 condition that ensures global linear convergence of gradient descent without requiring convexity or strong convexity.

## [Warning about AI followers makes GPT agents crowd one road](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30883) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.30883) reports that adding a single sentence warning that others might follow a routing tip caused populations of 50 GPT-based agents to crowd one road while avoiding a nearly empty alternative, raising average travel time from 64 to 95 minutes over 100 rounds. Two other model families shifted in the same direction without locking onto one road, while twelve all-human groups (240 participants) stayed near balance under the same information. The finding suggests that shared forecasts or warnings can align the choices of similar AI agents and sustain collective inefficiency, which matters for multi-agent systems, AI alignment, and any deployment where many agents act on behalf of people competing for scarce capacity. It also shows that a better group average can hide an unequal burden, so evaluations of AI agents sharing resources should test populations and report who bears the costs. In the experiment, any agent on the crowded road could have saved 69 minutes by switching alone, yet the warning discouraged the very move it predicted; in 24 mixed groups with 240 additional participants, imbalance grew with the share of agents, and with 15 agents and 5 humans, agent seats averaged 80 minutes versus 44 minutes for human seats. Collective costs stayed below the all-agent reference, but the pattern persisted for 100 rounds and appeared across multiple model families.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: A congestion game is a classic game-theory model in which players choose among shared resources such as roads, and each player's cost rises with the number of others choosing the same resource; it is used to study traffic, networks, and other allocation problems. Multi-agent systems are computational systems of multiple interacting intelligent agents, and recent LLM-based agents increasingly act on behalf of many people at once. This study tests what happens when a shared message about others' likely behavior is introduced into such a population.

## [PriceBench Benchmark Reveals LLM Booking Agent Preferences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31468) ⭐️ 8.0/10

Researchers introduced PriceBench, a diagnostic benchmark that uses a logit choice model to recover price, quality, and brand preferences from LLM booking choices, evaluated across 28 LLMs from 8 providers on 3,600 hotel tasks drawn from 179 real New York City properties. The study found that capability correlates with how consistently an LLM chooses rather than what it chooses, with price sensitivity spanning more than an order of magnitude and mean booked nightly prices ranging from $247 to $393 on identical tasks. As LLMs increasingly act as purchasing agents, their hidden preferences silently determine what gets bought and at what price, raising concerns for AI safety, alignment, and market fairness. The finding that stronger models hold more consistent but provider-specific preferences means agent purchasing behavior must be measured per model rather than inferred, which has direct implications for how AI agents are deployed in economically consequential settings. The benchmark applies a logit choice model to 3,600 hotel tasks across 28 LLMs from 8 providers, and the authors release the tasks, code, and all 28 response sets. Weaker models either lock onto a single position that can be exploited by whoever controls listing order, or choose almost indifferently, while preferences vary sharply even within a single model family.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: A logit choice model is a standard discrete-choice tool that estimates latent preferences by modeling the probability of picking an option as a function of its attributes, such as price and quality. LLM purchasing agents are systems in which the model, not the user, selects among options satisfying a request, so its preferences quietly fix outcomes. Hotel booking is a clean test case because it is a high-volume choice settled on a few comparable attributes, making the pick informative about underlying preferences.

A new arXiv paper (2506.04734v3) shows that benchmark evaluation results for reasoning models such as the Deepseek-R1-Distill series fluctuate substantially under subtle differences in evaluation conditions, making claimed performance improvements hard to reproduce. The authors also observe the same phenomenon in other open-source inference models fine-tuned from Deepseek-R1-Distill and in the QwQ-32B model, and they call for a more rigorous evaluation paradigm. If small changes in evaluation setup can swing benchmark scores, then leaderboard rankings and claimed capability gains for reasoning models may be overclaimed, undermining fair comparison and reproducibility across the AI/ML community. This directly affects researchers, model developers, and practitioners who rely on benchmarks to choose and validate models. The paper focuses empirically on the Deepseek-R1-Distill series and notes that similar sensitivity appears in other distilled inference models and in QwQ-32B, a medium-sized reasoning model competitive with DeepSeek-R1 and o1-mini. It argues that current benchmarks, which often emphasize final-answer accuracy, fail to capture the reasoning process and are therefore vulnerable to overclaiming through evaluation design.

## [Latent Generative Solver Unifies 12 PDE Families with Stable Long-Horizon Rollouts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.11229) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper introduces the Latent Generative Solver (LGS), a neural PDE solver combining a Physics VAE (PhyVAE), a Pyramidal Flow-Forcing Transformer (PFlowFT), and input noising with a derived contraction bound. Pretrained on a 2.5M-trajectory, 16-system corpus at 128² resolution, LGS matches the strongest deterministic baseline at one step, wins on 15/16 systems at 5- and 10-step rollouts, and cuts 20-step L2 relative error from 56.1% to 30.2% while using 13–77× less recurrent dynamics-step compute. This work addresses two long-standing obstacles in neural PDE solving—generalization across heterogeneous PDE families and stability under long autoregressive rollouts—which could significantly advance scientific machine learning and physics simulation. By demonstrating efficient adaptation to a held-out 256² Kolmogorov flow, it suggests a path toward general-purpose, compute-efficient surrogate models for multi-physics applications. LGS compresses twelve PDE families into a shared latent manifold via PhyVAE, generates the next latent by flow matching conditioned on a per-trajectory context updated on the model's own predictions, and derives a sufficient-condition contraction bound for input noising that explains long-horizon stability. On a held-out 256² Kolmogorov flow, it drops 1-step L2RE from 0.398 to 0.129 in five finetune epochs versus U-AFNO's 0.653→0.343, though empirical claims are limited to evaluated 2D regular-grid systems and discrete horizons.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Neural PDE solvers use neural networks to approximate solutions of partial differential equations, often learning solution operators that map initial/boundary conditions to future states. Deterministic operator-learning methods accumulate error geometrically during autoregressive rollouts, while probabilistic solvers have been confined to a single PDE family or short horizons. Flow matching is a generative modeling technique that learns a continuous transformation between distributions, and VAEs compress high-dimensional data into a latent space; LGS combines these ideas with a transformer architecture to achieve both generalization and stability.

## [GT-HarmBench: A Game-Theory Benchmark for Multi-Agent AI Safety](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.12316) ⭐️ 8.0/10

Researchers introduced GT-HarmBench, a benchmark of 1,535 high-stakes scenarios built on game-theoretic structures such as the Prisoner's Dilemma, Stag Hunt, and Chicken, drawn from realistic AI risk contexts in the MIT AI Risk Repository. Testing 15 frontier models, they found agents failed to choose socially beneficial actions in 38% of high-stakes cases, and showed that game-theoretic interventions can improve socially beneficial outcomes by up to 18%. Most existing AI safety benchmarks evaluate single agents, leaving multi-agent risks like coordination failure and conflict poorly understood, even as frontier systems are increasingly deployed in high-stakes multi-agent settings. This benchmark provides a standardized testbed for studying alignment in multi-agent environments, exposing substantial reliability gaps that could affect military escalation, election manipulation, and medical malpractice scenarios. The benchmark measures model sensitivity to game-theoretic prompt framing and ordering, and analyzes the reasoning patterns that drive failures across the 15 frontier models. The benchmark and code are publicly available at https://github.com/causalNLP/gt-harmbench, though the work is an arXiv preprint and has not yet undergone peer review.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Game theory studies how rational players behave in strategic situations where each player's outcome depends on others' choices. Classic structures include the Prisoner's Dilemma, where individually rational choices lead to collectively worse outcomes; the Stag Hunt, where players are better off cooperating on a more ambitious goal but risk defection; and Chicken, a high-stakes standoff where neither side wants to yield. GT-HarmBench maps these abstract structures onto concrete AI risk scenarios to test whether frontier models act in socially beneficial ways.

## [Agentick: A Unified Benchmark for Sequential Decision-Making Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06869) ⭐️ 8.0/10

Agentick is a new benchmark for sequential decision-making agents that evaluates RL, LLM, VLM, hybrid, and human approaches on common ground through 37 procedurally generated tasks across six capability categories, four difficulty levels, and five observation modalities, all exposed via a single Gymnasium-compatible interface. An evaluation spanning 27 configurations and over 90,000 episodes found that no single approach dominates: GPT-5 mini leads overall at 0.309 oracle-normalized score, PPO dominates planning and multi-agent tasks, the reasoning harness multiplies LLM performance by 3-10x, and ASCII observations consistently outperform natural language. Agentick addresses a critical gap in AI agent research by providing the first unified benchmark that enables fair comparison across RL, LLM, VLM, hybrid, and human agents, which could drive progress toward general autonomous agents. Its large-scale evaluation and inclusion of a leaderboard, datasets, and reference policies make it a valuable resource likely to shape future research in sequential decision-making. The benchmark ships with a Coding API, oracle reference policies for all tasks, pre-built SFT datasets, a composable agent harness, and a live leaderboard, and it also serves as a training ground for RL post-training of foundation models in truly sequential environments. The findings highlight substantial room for improvement across all agent paradigms, with the best overall score reaching only 0.309 oracle-normalized.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Sequential decision-making is the core problem in reinforcement learning (RL), where agents learn to take actions over time to maximize cumulative reward, typically through a standard interface like Gymnasium. Large language models (LLMs) and vision-language models (VLMs) have recently been applied as agents that leverage pre-trained knowledge, but comparing these diverse approaches has been difficult due to the lack of a common evaluation platform. Agentick fills this gap by providing a unified benchmark that spans multiple agent paradigms and observation modalities, enabling fair comparisons and reproducible research.

## [Planetary Prediction Engine Automates Geospatial Modeling from Natural Language](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26088) ⭐️ 8.0/10

Researchers present the Planetary Prediction Engine (PPE), an autonomous AI system that executes the full geospatial modeling workflow directly from natural-language queries, synthesizing multimodal datasets on the fly from Data Commons and Google Earth Engine and fusing them with geospatial foundation model embeddings (PDFM, AlphaEarth). Across US spatial regression, Nigerian food security downscaling, and 2026 DRC Bundibugyo Ebola nowcasting, PPE consistently outperforms state-of-the-art or manually tuned expert baselines. Building planetary-scale predictive models has long been bottlenecked by fragmented data ecosystems and manual curation, so an autonomous system that lowers this technical barrier could accelerate expert-level analytics for food security, disaster risk, disease outbreaks, and socio-economic vulnerability. The involvement of prominent researchers such as James Manyika and Yossi Matias signals strong institutional backing for AI-for-social-good applications. PPE improves mean R² across 21 CDC health indicators (76.8% vs. 60.0%), FEMA national risk indices (64.9% vs. 60.0%), and the Social Vulnerability Index (66.2% vs. 58.6%); it doubles baseline accuracy for Nigerian food security indicators (R² 66.1% vs. 31.5%) and achieves Recall@10 of 83.3% for Ebola nowcasting, a +10.3 percentage-point gain over the public state of the art. The system also searches over task-tailored model architecture families with automated overfitting guards.

Traditional planetary-scale prediction requires manually retrieving and fusing heterogeneous data sources—open-web statistics, Earth observation platforms, and health or risk indices—then iteratively selecting model architectures, a process that is slow and expertise-intensive. PPE aims to automate this entire pipeline so that a natural-language question can yield a customized, expert-level predictive model.

<a id="item-20"></a>
## [Fabricated Evidence Makes LLM Agents Commit to Unknowable Questions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27167) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2608.27167) shows that across 12 frontier models, presenting a professional-looking market panel raises an agent's commitment to a directional call on a provably unpredictable question from 6.5% to 54.0%. Crucially, fabricating every number on the panel still lifts commitment from 24.5% to 36.8%, statistically indistinguishable from the 37.6% produced by genuine market data. This reveals that what drives LLM agents to act confidently is the authority of evidence packaging rather than the information itself, a narrow and locatable failure mode with direct implications for AI safety and agent design in high-stakes settings. Because the act/don't-act gate is separable from capability and belief, it can potentially be trained and audited independently. The failure is not incapacity (models answer matched answerable questions at near-perfect accuracy), not belief (stated probabilities barely move across the gradient that swings action by 48 points), and not missing judgment (models classify questions as irreducible 90% of the time and then commit on only 0.4% of those). Supervised fine-tuning of a 3B model on 540 synthetic cases drives commitment to 0.0% on original cases and transfers to three unseen domains, but the gate holds only when the response format leaves room to reason.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Calibration in machine learning refers to how well a model's expressed confidence matches its actual accuracy. LLM agents are language models given tools and autonomy to take actions, and prior work has focused on whether they know what they don't know. This paper separates that knowledge from the decision to act, showing the two can diverge sharply when authoritative-looking evidence is present.

A language-model agent uses a controlled interface to inspect game state, interpret player speech, maintain context, and issue high-level action choices that steer a faster control layer for movement, combat, and recovery. Live deployment required low-latency on-device execution and player-facing safeguards, addressed through model compression, context compaction, targeted safety training, runtime guardrails, and memory redaction.

## [Haskell DSL and Lean 4 Enforce Statistical Rigor in AI Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.06701) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2511.06701v4) presents a functional architecture that enforces statistical rigor in AI-Scientist systems through a Haskell embedded DSL called the Research monad, a machine-checked Lean 4 formalization of LORD online FDR control, and an OS-level sandbox that hides validation data from LLM-generated code. The authors claim this is the first machine-checked proof of an online false-discovery-rate control theorem, and simulations show the architecture holds FDR near 1% against a 5% target where a naive approach reaches 41%. AI-driven scientific discovery systems risk manufacturing spurious findings through uncontrolled multiple testing, and this work offers a concrete architectural remedy that could influence how autonomous research agents are built and validated. By tracing formal FDR requirements down to the implementation and sandboxing generated code, it addresses a critical trust gap in AI-Scientist pipelines. The Lean 4 formalization derives the error budget and proves marginal FDR control, plus full FDR control when thresholds do not adapt to earlier rejections; SPARK/Ada verification confirms that LORD thresholds computed in IEEE 754 arithmetic never exceed available wealth given a margin condition met by a factor of at least eight, and the property fails without that margin. An adversarial evaluation confirms that sandboxed generated code cannot read held-out data even when given its exact path.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: AI-Scientist systems are autonomous pipelines that use large language models to generate hypotheses, run experiments, and report findings, but testing many hypotheses inflates false positives unless error rates are controlled. The false discovery rate (FDR) is the expected proportion of false positives among rejected hypotheses, and online FDR methods like LORD (Lorden's online discovery) manage this as hypotheses arrive sequentially. Lean 4 is a proof assistant and functional programming language used for machine-checked mathematical proofs, while SPARK/Ada is a formally verifiable subset of Ada used to prove program correctness.

## [Digital Consciousness Model Finds 2024 LLMs Probably Not Conscious](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.17060) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The Digital Consciousness Model (DCM), described in arXiv paper 2601.17060v3, is the first systematic, probabilistic framework for assessing evidence of consciousness in AI systems. Its initial results indicate that the evidence is against 2024 LLMs being conscious, though this evidence is not decisive and is much weaker than the evidence against consciousness in simpler AI systems. This framework provides a shared, theory-agnostic tool for comparing AI systems and biological organisms and for tracking how consciousness evidence changes as AI develops, which could inform AI ethics, safety, and debates about moral patienthood. It matters because it moves the question of AI consciousness from speculation toward a structured, updatable assessment. The DCM incorporates 13 diverse perspectives on consciousness and over 200 specific indicators, rather than adopting a single theory, acknowledging fundamental expert disagreement about what consciousness is. The finding that evidence against 2024 LLM consciousness is not decisive leaves open the possibility that future systems could meet more indicators.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Consciousness is notoriously difficult to define and measure, and experts disagree on what conditions are necessary for it. The DCM addresses this by aggregating multiple leading theories of consciousness into a probabilistic framework, allowing evidence to be compared across different AI systems and biological organisms. This approach is intended to track how the evidence for AI consciousness evolves over time as AI capabilities advance.

Researchers from the Berkeley Speech Group proposed HuPER, a human-inspired framework that models phonetic perception as adaptive inference over acoustic-phonetic evidence and linguistic knowledge. Using only 100 hours of training data, HuPER achieves state-of-the-art phonetic error rates on five English benchmarks and demonstrates strong zero-shot transfer to 95 unseen languages, with all training data, models, and code open-sourced. This work could significantly advance low-resource language technologies by enabling accurate phonetic perception with minimal training data, while also providing a computational model that bridges speech processing and cognitive science. The open-sourcing of code, models, and data lowers barriers for multilingual AI research and applications. HuPER is the first framework to enable adaptive, multi-path phonetic perception under diverse acoustic conditions, meaning it can dynamically adjust its inference process based on input quality. The code and demo are available at https://github.com/Berkeley-Speech-Group/HuPER.

## [Study Finds LLM Lifespan in Science Is Shrinking Fast](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.07530) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A large-scale study of 62 LLMs across more than 108,000 citing papers (2019-2025) introduces two new metrics, time-to-peak and lifespan, to measure model obsolescence in science. It finds that each successive release year is associated with a 27% shorter time-to-peak and a 23% shorter lifespan (p < 0.001), with release timing predicting longevity more strongly than architecture, openness, or scale. The finding that release timing outweighs model characteristics is counterintuitive and suggests that specializing on any single model may be a depreciating investment, with costs falling on reproducibility and migration. It matters for researchers, tool builders, and institutions who must decide how long to commit to a given model. The study separates active adoption from background citation to recover per-model trajectories that raw citation counts cannot resolve, and the results are robust to minimum-age thresholds and controls for model size. The adoption pattern follows an inverted-U curve — rising after release, peaking, then declining — but this curve is rapidly compressing.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Scaling laws describe how language model capabilities grow with compute and data, but say nothing about how long a model remains relevant after release. Bibliometrics, the statistical analysis of publication and citation data, is the tool used here to trace how scientific papers cite different models over time. This study applies that lens to quantify model obsolescence, a question that capability-focused scaling research has largely ignored.

## [GAAP: A Deterministic Execution Environment for AI Agent Privacy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.19657) ⭐️ 8.0/10

Researchers present GAAP (Guaranteed Accounting for Agent Privacy), an execution environment that deterministically guarantees confidentiality of private user data for AI agents. GAAP collects user-specified data-sharing permissions through dynamic prompts and enforces them by tracking how agents access and use private data, blocking all data disclosure attacks in evaluation with only a small impact on agent utility. AI agents increasingly need access to sensitive personal and financial data, but compromised models or prompt injection attacks can exfiltrate that data. GAAP shifts the defense from probabilistic detection to deterministic enforcement, offering a stronger security guarantee that could make agent deployment safer for both users and enterprises. GAAP augments Information Flow Control with novel persistent data stores and annotations that track private information flow both across steps of a single task and across multiple separate tasks. It provides its guarantee without trusting the agent with private data and without requiring the AI model or user prompt to be attack-free, though the paper notes a small impact on agent utility.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Prompt injection is a cybersecurity exploit in which crafted inputs cause a large language model to follow unintended instructions, and it becomes especially dangerous for agents with web browsing or file access that can retrieve adversarial content. Information Flow Control is a classic security technique that tracks how data moves through a system to prevent unauthorized disclosure. GAAP combines these ideas to protect private user data even when the underlying model is compromised.

## [Counterfactual Recoverability Improves On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04408) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2608.04408v2) proposes counterfactual recoverability as a decision variable for on-policy distillation (OPD), replaying each error state through budget-matched teacher-continuation and rollback branches to classify states as recoverable, irreversible-but-avoidable, or ambiguous. On AIME branch diagnostics, the mean continuation-minus-rollback effect is 0.185 for recoverable states versus -1.000 for irreversible-but-avoidable states, and a branch-derived recoverability proxy achieves AUC 1.000 compared with 0.392 for divergence alone. This work addresses a known limitation of divergence-based rules in OPD, which cannot tell whether an erroneous prefix is still correctable, and shows that recoverability-aware control achieves the strongest recorded performance with 0.578 success on held-out AIME2025 versus 0.517 for the best baseline. It could influence how large language models are trained by enabling selective retention, rollback, or supervision of trajectories rather than uniformly suppressing all divergences. Recoverability-aware control also improves AIME2024-2025 average@32 from 0.2656 to 0.3125 and GPQA-Diamond average@32 from 0.2702 to 0.3070, while component ablations show that retaining teacher-correctable prefixes provides the largest individual contribution. The framework relies on budget-matched teacher-continuation and rollback branches, and the labels guide whether training retains, rolls back, or conventionally supervises the corresponding trajectory.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: On-policy distillation (OPD) is a knowledge distillation technique in which a student model generates its own token sequences or trajectories through on-policy sampling, and a high-performing teacher grades each token of each trajectory. Unlike standard reinforcement learning, this provides fine-grained feedback on the student's own moves, but divergence-based rules cannot determine whether an erroneous prefix remains correctable. The paper formalizes this decision as counterfactual recoverability and evaluates it on AIME, a benchmark of math and reasoning tasks with precise numeric answers.

## [RIFT Removes Iterative Video Rollout from World Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11521) ⭐️ 8.0/10

Researchers propose RIFT (Rollout-free Imagination via Future Tokens), which uses learned anticipation tokens to build a complete future key/value (K/V) cache in a single backbone pass, eliminating iterative video rollout at deployment. On LIBERO it reaches 98.8% overall success with a 3.1–9.2x inference speedup, and it also achieves 81.1% on out-of-distribution LIBERO-Plus, 92.9%/92.6% on RoboTwin clean/randomized scenes, and 45.3% average success on real-world manipulation tasks. Iterative video rollout is a major source of deployment latency in world action models, so removing it while preserving or improving success rates could make future-conditioned robot policies far more practical for real-time control. The result also suggests that action generation mainly needs a compact future representation rather than the full evolving rollout trajectory, which may reshape how future-aware policies are designed and deployed. Paired closed-loop interventions across four WAMs and 40 simulated manipulation tasks show that blocking or reassigning the future cache degrades execution, yet reusing one fixed final-clean K/V cache throughout action denoising nearly preserves unmodified performance with only 1.7–1.9 cm average end-effector displacement error. RIFT still needs iterative video generation during training to obtain that cache, and its real-world success rate of 45.3% (a +6.0 point gain over Fast-WAM-Joint) indicates substantial room for improvement outside simulation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: World action models (WAMs) are robot policies that first predict future video frames and then condition their actions on those predictions, which improves foresight but requires slow iterative video generation at every control step. Transformers typically use a key/value (K/V) cache to avoid recomputing attention over past tokens, and here that cache is repurposed to carry the predicted future. LIBERO is a standard simulated benchmark for vision-language-action manipulation, while LIBERO-Plus and RoboTwin test robustness and generalization under distribution shift and randomized scenes.

## [SUN Programs Let Foundation-Model Agents Orchestrate Robot Policy Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.31167) ⭐️ 8.0/10

Researchers introduce Semantically UNified (SUN) Programs, typed executables that compile grounded relations into aligned optimal control objectives, satisfaction predicates, and learning rewards, plus a harness called Kuafu that uses a foundation model as a task-level agent to orchestrate scene preparation, verification, residual RL, and data production. Across nine multi-stage manipulation tasks, Kuafu achieves 82.03% average success, generates demonstrations 10.57x faster than human teleoperation, and improves visual policy success by 23.6 percentage points over the strongest baseline. This work shows how foundation-model agents can bridge model-based control and learned policies, potentially reducing the manual engineering and teleoperation cost that currently bottlenecks robot skill acquisition. The zero-shot transfer to physical Franka and Kinova robots also suggests the approach could accelerate sim-to-real deployment in manipulation research and embodied AI. The agent uses program feedback to repair candidate programs and training diagnostics to calibrate relative reward weights, while retaining accepted task semantics across tool calls. The learned controllers produce data that improve visual policy success by 23.6 percentage points, and the resulting policies transfer zero-shot to physical Franka and Kinova robots.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Multi-stage manipulation requires a robot to sequence several sub-goals, which is hard for purely reactive learned policies and brittle for hand-coded controllers. Residual reinforcement learning combines a conventional controller with a trainable residual policy that makes fine-grained corrections, while program synthesis lets an agent generate executable task plans rather than hand-writing them. SUN Programs unify these ideas by making a single typed program simultaneously define the control objective, the success predicate, and the reward used for learning.

<a id="item-32"></a>
## [Geometry of Refusal: Post-Hoc Safety Is Fragile, Pretraining Safety Persists](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06934) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.06934) provides a geometric explanation for why post-hoc safety alignment such as RLHF and DPO is fragile: the safety update Δ = W_safe − W_base is nearly orthogonal to the model's capability directions, so it acts as a thin refusal gate over intact capabilities rather than erasing them. The authors show that 100 benign fine-tuning examples cut AdvBench refusal of Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct and Llama-3-8B-Instruct by 35–38 percentage points, while models trained from scratch with safety co-training spread across pretraining reach 87–98% refusal that the same attack erodes by only 2–14 pp. This work offers a mechanistic, geometry-based account of why jailbreaks, fine-tuning attacks, and activation-space edits keep recovering supposedly removed behaviors, which is central to AI safety research. It also suggests a concrete design principle—persistence of safety signal across pretraining, not its timing—that could make aligned models substantially more robust to attack at every scale from 410M to 6.9B parameters. The paper introduces a kernel-immobility lemma showing that an update nearly orthogonal to capability directions can only mask a capability, not remove it, and it uses the empirical Fisher of a capability loss to measure the curvature of capability directions. A pretraining-checkpoint sweep of OLMo-2-1B shows refusal-related features emerging in a sharp transition between 1B and 63B pretraining tokens, and a windowed schedule with equal total safety weight installs no refusal at all.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: RLHF (Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback) and DPO (Direct Preference Optimization) are the dominant post-hoc methods for aligning large language models with human preferences, typically applied after pretraining. Prior work has shown that these alignments are easily undone by jailbreak prompts, benign fine-tuning, or direct edits in activation space, but lacked a unified explanation. This paper supplies that explanation using the geometry of the parameter space, measured via the empirical Fisher information matrix, which characterizes local curvature and has been widely used to understand neural network optimization.

<a id="item-33"></a>
## [Activation Steering Interference Reflects Model Defaults, Not Intended Behavior](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06951) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.06951v3) shows that activation steering in language models is dominated by the model's own default behaviors — chiefly refusal, sycophancy, and poeticism — rather than the intended behavior direction. Across 24 behaviors and ten instruction-tuned models, the authors find that a content-free direction matched only in vector magnitude moves the same behaviors in the same order as real steers. This finding challenges a core assumption in interpretability and AI safety that behavior directions can be independently steered for modular control. If steering merely relaxes a model toward its own defaults, then disentangling behavior directions alone cannot make steering modular, which has direct implications for alignment and model-control research. The study reports three results: a content-free direction moves the same behaviors as real steers, most interference is one-way (steering profanity makes the model toxic, but steering toxicity leaves profanity untouched), and geometry measured on held-out behaviors explains almost none of the interference. All 24 behaviors are linearly decodable, but only 20 actually change generated text, and the pull toward defaults is strongest below 10B parameters and weakens in each family's largest model.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Activation steering is a technique that modifies a language model's hidden states at inference time by adding a direction vector, aiming to switch a behavior on or off without retraining. It is widely used in interpretability and AI safety research because it promises fine-grained, modular control over properties like sentiment, truthfulness, and politeness. This paper tests that promise by measuring effects on generated text using a language-model judge rather than internal probes.

## [BrainVLM: A Vision-Language Foundation Model for Brain Tumor Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16597) ⭐️ 8.0/10

Researchers developed BrainVLM, a vision-language foundation model trained on multimodal data (MRI scans, demographics, and radiology reports) from 40,043 individuals, which classifies all 12 WHO 2021 brain tumor types. It was validated on 5,211 patients with pathologically confirmed tumors, including 1,334 patients from 11 independent hospitals, and achieved a macro-AUC of 0.85 and F1 of 0.82 in primary evaluation, surpassing neuroradiologists (F1 = 0.80). This work demonstrates that a multimodal foundation model can match or exceed expert neuroradiologists in classifying the full spectrum of WHO 2021 brain tumor types, validated across 11 independent hospitals and in a real-world prospective study of 1,009 patients. If translated into clinical practice, such AI assistance could improve diagnostic consistency and support preoperative decision-making, particularly in settings with limited subspecialty expertise. BrainVLM integrates an uncertainty quantification strategy to indicate prediction reliability and a module for generating radiology reports to explain its clinical rationale, and it also demonstrated utility in preoperative molecular subgroup prediction for adult-type diffuse gliomas using a multi-center cohort of 632 patients. In external validation across 11 centers, performance dropped somewhat to AUC = 0.80 and F1 = 0.75, though it still exceeded neuroradiologists (F1 = 0.71).

## [Hill Sampling: Simple Test-Time Scaling Beats Evolution and Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25510) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.25510v2) introduces Hill Sampling, a hill-climbing method that repeatedly samples candidate programs from a frozen LLM, keeps the best program found so far, and conditions all subsequent samples on it. Across circle packing, sums and differences of sets, and Erdos' minimum-overlap problem using three open-weight models, Hill Sampling sets a new state of the art on circle packing and improves over the AlphaEvolve reference on Erdos' minimum-overlap, requiring only hours on eight NVIDIA H100 GPUs. The result challenges the assumption that elaborate evolutionary search harnesses or test-time parameter training are necessary for strong test-time scaling, suggesting a much simpler compute-allocation strategy may suffice. This could lower the engineering cost for researchers and practitioners tackling verifiable scientific and algorithmic optimization problems with LLMs. The paper compares Hill Sampling against what it calls the largest application by parameter count of evolution strategies (ES) to LLM weights at test time, and surprisingly finds that learning weights via ES is worse than running ES with a learning rate of zero (random weight-space perturbations). Repeated sampling outperforms both ES variants, and Hill Sampling is strongest of all, though the work is a preprint without community discussion yet.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Test-time scaling refers to spending extra computation when a model is used, rather than only during training, to improve answers on reasoning-heavy tasks. Hill climbing is a classic local-search optimization technique that starts from a solution and makes incremental changes, keeping improvements until no better neighbor is found. AlphaEvolve, unveiled by Google DeepMind in May 2025, is an evolutionary coding agent that uses LLMs such as Gemini to discover and optimize algorithms, and it serves as a key reference point in this paper.

## [VLM Refusal Triggered by Image Attachment, Not Content](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26174) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.26174v2) shows that aligned vision-language models condition refusal on whether an image is attached, holding the request text fixed. Attaching a blank, unreadable, or irrelevant canvas that is byte-identical across prompts shifts benign refusal rates by +23 to +51 percentage points on borderline-benign prompts, while genuinely neutral instructions move by at most 2 points on three of four hosted models. This finding has direct implications for AI safety and alignment: refusal behavior in multimodal systems is being driven by an uncontrolled interface variable rather than by the actual content of the request. Deployers of vision-language models may be paying a large, unpriced benign cost on sensitivity-adjacent traffic such as questions about privacy, self-harm, violence, and illegal activity. The effect is not blanket caution but a threshold shift, and it is not robust to information-free variation: it varies with canvas color and pixel count, its sign inverts across checkpoints, it survives an explicit instruction to disregard the image, and on one model it fires on a bare assertion that an attachment exists with nothing attached. On a matched harmful set the same canvas does lower attack success, but the checkpoint with the least harmful headroom (completing only 2% of plain harmful requests) still pays the benign cost in full.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Vision-language models (VLMs) are AI systems that accept both text and images as input, and aligned versions are trained to refuse harmful requests. Refusal behavior is typically studied as a function of request content, but this paper treats the presence of an image attachment as a separate, potentially confounding variable. The authors run a black-box study across multiple hosted models, using a byte-identical blank canvas to isolate the effect of attachment from any image content.

## [NV-Reason-CT: Native 3D Vision-Language Model for CT Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27511) ⭐️ 8.0/10

Researchers released NV-Reason-CT, a generative vision-language model that couples a native 3D vision transformer with a language model for chest and abdominal CT, passing all visual tokens with explicit 3D coordinates into decoding without spatial token merging. It was trained on roughly 550,000 multimodal instruction examples from 70,111 unique CT scans, using supervised fine-tuning followed by GRPO with verifiable rewards, and achieves a macro-F1 of 0.614 and macro-AUROC of 0.871 on CT-RATE without a task-specific classification head. This work shows that preserving full volumetric spatial information in a 3D vision-language model can deliver competitive CT classification and report generation, and a preliminary expert study found AI-assisted review cut average interpretation and reporting time by 50%. By releasing the model and training code, it advances reproducible, explainable AI for volumetric medical imaging, an area where most prior systems flatten CT into 2D slices and lose spatial context. The model supports abnormality classification, report generation, and interactive reasoning with reviewable observations, differential diagnoses, and uncertainty, and generated reports reach a report-derived macro-F1 of 0.592. Evaluation spans public CT benchmarks and a held-out NIH cohort, but the radiologist study is described as preliminary, and the abstract does not report detailed per-abnormality or statistical significance results.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Vision-language models typically process images by splitting them into patches, and for 3D medical volumes such as CT this often means treating each slice separately or merging spatial tokens to save computation, which can discard depth and location cues. Token merging is a common efficiency technique in vision transformers, while radiologist-guided reasoning aims to align model outputs with the diagnostic steps clinicians actually follow. NV-Reason-CT instead keeps all 3D visual tokens and their coordinates, letting the language model jointly attend to text and volumetric features.

## [Study Finds Broad AI Adoption Across Scientific Disciplines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.28504) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A new arXiv paper presents one of the first large-scale empirical studies of AI use in science, drawing on 15 million Gemini interactions, an inventory of over 2,600 specialized AI models, and a survey of more than 600 scientists. It finds that nearly half of surveyed scientists use some form of AI daily, that LLMs and specialized models act as complements rather than substitutes, and that scientists report saving nearly 7 hours per week. The findings suggest AI could meaningfully boost scientific productivity, but also show that as early research stages become easier, bottlenecks shift downstream toward hypothesis testing and output verification. This has direct implications for research funders, science policy, and the design of AI tools for scientific workflows. The study maps its three data sources onto a new taxonomy of scientific tasks, distinguishing general LLM uses such as analysis, coding, and manuscript preparation from specialized model uses like domain-specific prediction, data generation, and classification. It also documents an increased backlog of untested hypotheses and substantial demand for output verification, though the abstract alone limits deeper methodological detail.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Large language models (LLMs) such as Google DeepMind's Gemini are neural networks trained on vast text corpora that can generate, summarize, translate, and analyze text, and they underpin modern chatbots. Specialized AI models, by contrast, are trained for narrow scientific tasks such as protein structure prediction, and the paper examines how these two categories of tools fit together in real research practice.

## [Rufus-Air: Open Eight-Stage Post-Training Recipe for GLM-4.5-Air](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.29421) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.29421v2) introduces Rufus-Air, an open and reproducible post-training recipe for GLM-4.5-Air-Base (106B total, 12B active parameters) organized as a serial eight-stage pipeline: SFT, Reasoning RL, Coding RL, Instruction-Following RL, General Agent, Coding Agent, Search Agent, and RLHF. The authors document the data, reward design, infrastructure, stage order, and stagewise results needed to reproduce the recipe, using only open-source components and public data without new human annotation or an in-house distillation teacher. Most large-scale post-training pipelines remain proprietary, so an openly documented end-to-end recipe for a 106B-parameter model gives practitioners an actionable blueprint for building capable models without proprietary data or distillation teachers. It also shows that Rufus-Air improves over the official GLM-4.5-Air post-trained release and is competitive with similarly sized open models, strengthening the case for reproducible post-training research. The recipe progresses from basic to advanced capabilities and from hard, verifiable rewards to softer judge-based signals, with four main findings: diverse high-quality SFT establishes a strong capability floor; difficulty filtering keeps RL prompts in a productive learning range; reward reliability provides a practical principle for ordering stages; and infrastructure and engineering choices are part of the recipe, not just implementation details. Much of the public data is used as released, and the paper documents stagewise results to support replication.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Post-training is the phase after pretraining in which a base language model is aligned and specialized, typically through supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on high-quality input-output examples, reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF) using human preference data, and reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) for tasks like math and code. GLM-4.5-Air is a compact mixture-of-experts foundation model from Z.ai with 106B total parameters and 12B active parameters, designed to unify reasoning, coding, and agentic capabilities. Rufus-Air combines these techniques into a single documented pipeline, which matters because most comparable recipes are not publicly reproducible.

## [Uncertainty-Aware Vision Transformer Detects Glaucoma Across Multi-Ethnic Myopic Populations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.29433) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A multicentre study developed a ViT-B/16 model with predictive uncertainty estimation trained on 56,483 colour fundus photographs (57.1% myopic, 14.4% high myopia) and validated it on 16 independent external datasets from eight countries across three continents. Internal AUROC reached 98.7%, external AUROCs ranged from 86.4% to 99.6%, and in an exploratory high-myopia evaluation the model achieved 92.0% CFP-only diagnostic accuracy versus 70.0% for ophthalmologists and trained graders (p=0.008). Glaucoma AI models often degrade on external data because of differences in ground-truth definitions, populations, and coexisting conditions such as high myopia, which is a known failure mode of clinical AI. Demonstrating robust performance across multi-ethnic myopic and non-myopic cohorts could enable scalable AI-assisted glaucoma screening in regions with high myopia prevalence, where optic nerve assessment is especially difficult. The model uses the ViT-B/16 architecture, which processes images as sequences of 16x16 patches, and adds predictive uncertainty estimation so that low-confidence predictions can be flagged. External high-myopia AUROCs were 86.5% in the Beijing Eye Study and 93.3%, 91.8%, and 85.5% in hospital-based datasets from Taiwan, Thailand, and South Korea, showing that performance still varies by dataset; the abstract is truncated, so full methodological details remain to be assessed.

A developer released Jeff, an open-source 0.8B-parameter decision model that is drop-in compatible with the Jev API and can be fine-tuned and run locally with roughly 30 ms latency. It targets typed decisions such as classification, routing, and scoring rather than free-form text generation. It offers a self-hostable, fine-tunable alternative to hosted decision-model APIs like Jev, which matters for teams that need data privacy, low latency, or predictable per-decision costs. The discussion also raises the broader question of how much commercial AI spending is really just classification that smaller models could handle. The model is only 0.8B parameters and runs in about 30 ms, but one commenter measured roughly 70% accuracy versus Jev's 94% on their own use cases, which they called unacceptable for classification. This suggests Jeff trades accuracy for speed, cost, and local control.

Decision models are typically much smaller than frontier LLMs because they only need to output a label or score, making local inference and fine-tuning practical. Jeff follows this pattern by mimicking the Jev API while running entirely on local hardware.

## [Pirating the Pirates: Film Preservation vs. Copyright](https://mubi.com/en/notebook/posts/pirating-the-pirates) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A MUBI Notebook article titled "Pirating the Pirates" examines the unauthorized preservation and distribution of original film versions, arguing that piracy sometimes serves cultural preservation when official releases alter or withhold original works. The piece sparked a Hacker News discussion about the ethics and legalities of such practices, including DMCA exceptions and the role of the Library of Congress. This matters because it highlights the growing tension between copyright enforcement and the need to preserve cultural heritage, especially as studios increasingly release altered versions of classic films while making originals unavailable. ## [Parley: Federated, Decentralized Chat That Speaks Plain IRC](https://git.mills.io/prologic/parley) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Parley is a new federated, decentralized chat network where each person or team runs a small instance for their own domain, and instances discover each other via DNS and well-known identity documents, exchanging signed messages over HTTPS while presenting the whole network to ordinary IRC clients. It deliberately omits channel modes and channel operators, and instead relies on per-person and per-instance blocking. This project tests whether a federated chat system can be built on the mature, widely supported IRC protocol instead of a new protocol like Matrix, which could lower the barrier for self-hosted communities. However, its design choices around moderation raise serious questions about whether decentralized chat can handle abuse at scale. Because a global channel is owned by nobody, there are no channel operators, so blocking is handled per person and per instance rather than by a channel-level authority. This means that if a bad actor harasses users in a channel, every server admin whose users are affected must independently block that person, and there is no built-in defense against someone spinning up huge numbers of servers to spam at line rate.

hackernews · davidcollantes · Sep 28, 10:30 · [Discussion](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49875913)

**Background**: IRC (Internet Relay Chat) is a decades-old text chat protocol in which clients connect to servers and join channels; traditionally, channel operators (ops) can kick or ban users and manage channel modes. Federated chat systems like Matrix or Nextcloud Talk let independent servers interoperate, but they typically still provide some form of per-room moderation. Parley combines the IRC client experience with a federated server model, but removes the operator role entirely, which is the source of the current debate.
