## [Externalized CPDAG Summaries Improve LLM Causal Deduction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31071) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper proposes Structured Thinking, a two-turn pipeline that first externalizes a typed, schema-constrained CPDAG summary and then answers causal queries against that graph state. On the Corr2Cause full test, it raises Qwen3.5-27B from 73.0 to 86.4 F1(Yes) over a strong PC-instruction baseline, a +13.4 pp gain (McNemar p=2.4×10⁻⁶; bootstrap 95% CI [+8.4, +18.6]). The results suggest a general design principle for LLM reasoning: externalize the latent object that defines the label, constrain its form, and verify that downstream answers actually use it. This could improve reliability in causal inference and other structured reasoning tasks where free-form chain-of-thought tends to collapse into local pattern matching. A PC-scaffolded two-turn prose control reaches only 67.6 F1, showing that a detailed PC scaffold plus schema-free prose intermediate is insufficient; scrambling the emitted CPDAG costs 12.0 pp F1, and a full-split audit shows close agreement with the reference CPDAG (ID skeleton F1 0.960; exact match 75.9%). The same pattern holds on Qwen3.6-27B, Paraphrase-OOD, and GPT-5.4-mini, though across three full-ID seeds the mean gain is +8.1 ± 5.3 pp.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Corr2Cause asks whether a causal claim holds in every directed acyclic graph (DAG) compatible with observed correlations and conditional independencies. Because many DAGs can encode the same conditional-independence patterns, they form a Markov equivalence class, which is uniquely represented by a completed partially directed acyclic graph (CPDAG). The label in Corr2Cause is defined by a CPDAG query, but free-form chain-of-thought often collapses this Markov-equivalence-class problem into local pattern matching.

## [Code-Based Skills Nearly Triple NetHack Agent Progress](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31076) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.31076) introduces CodeHack, a library of reusable code-based skills with natural-language descriptions for the game NetHack, and systematically compares language agents restricted to primitive actions against those using semantic skills alone or combined with primitives. Across zero-shot prompting, supervised fine-tuning, and reinforcement learning, skill-based agents nearly tripled game progression and cut inference cost per episode by 86%, while in RL they achieved a 7.2x larger average gain in dungeon level over the same training budget. The results suggest that supplying a fixed library of code-based skills is a cheap and effective way to make language agents more capable and efficient in long-horizon environments, which could inform how future agent frameworks are designed. Keeping primitive actions available as a fallback also addresses the well-known problem of leaky abstractions, offering a practical recipe for balancing productivity and flexibility. The study uses NetHack, a long-horizon roguelike, and evaluates agents in three settings: zero-shot prompting, supervised fine-tuning, and reinforcement learning. Combining skills with primitives retains most of the benefit while preserving a path back to low-level actions, and the authors release CodeHack together with training and evaluation code.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Language agents powered by large language models often struggle in environments that require long sequences of low-level actions, because repeatedly choosing individual actions is slow and error-prone. Code-based abstractions let an agent invoke reusable skills written in code, so the code handles recurring local decisions while the language model decides which skills to use and how to combine them. NetHack is a classic open-source roguelike first released in 1987, known for its long-horizon, procedurally generated gameplay, which makes it a popular but difficult benchmark for AI agents.

## [OmouAI Combines LLMs and Argumentation to Curb Sycophancy in Policy Debates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31078) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.31078v1) introduces OmouAI, an interactive deliberation system that pairs large language models with computational argumentation and simulated personas representing stakeholders, domain experts, or devil's advocates. Users can contest, add, and revise arguments within a shared argumentation framework, and arguments are evaluated with deterministic argumentative semantics against external goals such as the UN Sustainable Development Goals. Sycophancy—LLMs' tendency to agree with users rather than challenge them—is a serious risk in high-stakes settings like public policy deliberation, and OmouAI's design aims to preserve human oversight while producing faithful, goal-grounded explanations. If it works, this approach could inform how AI-assisted civic deliberation, expert advisory tools, and other human-AI decision systems are built. Each simulated persona generates its own arguments, which are aggregated into a single shared argumentation framework that the human user can edit, and the system uses deterministic argumentative semantics rather than LLM judgment to evaluate arguments against external goals. The paper is an arXiv preprint and does not yet report peer review, deployment results, or community discussion.

Sycophancy in LLMs refers to their tendency to confirm users' beliefs and adopt their framing instead of prioritizing accuracy or critical challenge, which is especially dangerous in policy contexts. ## [LIFT Transfers Reasoning Vectors from LLMs to VLMs Without Retraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31140) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.31140) introduces LIFT (Language-side reasonIng Facilitation and Transfer), a lightweight vector-intervention method that transfers reasoning capability from a base LLM to its aligned VLM without retraining the backbone. LIFT defines Reasoning Vectors as answer-token hidden-state differences between a Reasoner path with an explicit reasoning trace and a Solver path without it, then injects these vectors into the language-side activations of the target VLM, optionally with learnable adaptation while keeping the VLM frozen. Multimodal scaling is known to degrade the language-side reasoning ability of base LLMs, and LIFT offers a training-free way to partially recover it, which could lower the cost of building stronger VLMs. The finding that LLM-derived vectors consistently outperform VLM-derived vectors suggests the base LLM remains a better source of reasoning signals, with implications for how future VLMs are aligned and fine-tuned. LIFT was evaluated on two VLMs across six reasoning benchmarks, comparing Reasoning Vectors extracted from the base LLM versus from the aligned VLM under matched protocols; LLM-derived vectors consistently won. Further analysis shows the vectors influence intermediate reasoning behavior rather than merely altering final answers, and the source code is promised to be released soon.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Vision-Language Models (VLMs) are typically built by attaching visual modules to a pretrained Large Language Model (LLM) and then aligning the two modalities through additional training. This process, often called multimodal scaling or instruction tuning, can cause the model to lose some of the text-only reasoning ability it originally had. Prior work such as training-free model merging has tried to mitigate this degradation, but LIFT takes a different route by extracting and injecting reasoning directions as vectors rather than merging weights.

## [Neural State Prediction Reduces Shortcut Learning in EEG Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31167) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Neural State Prediction (NSP), a latent-predictive framework that constrains both the prediction target and the available context to reduce shortcut learning in EEG foundation models. NSP pretrains on 2.2 million EEG segments from TUEG and achieves 63.94 macro balanced accuracy across 14 datasets on EEG-FM-Bench, exceeding the strongest baseline by 2.35 percentage points. Shortcut learning is a major obstacle to building generalizable EEG foundation models, and NSP's approach of jointly designing latent targets and their context could improve transfer across diverse clinical and cognitive tasks. This matters for researchers and clinicians who rely on EEG-based diagnosis, sleep staging, emotion recognition, and brain-computer interfaces. NSP uses a Target Encoder updated by exponential moving average (EMA) to define latent supervision, applies identity residualization to remove additive effects of channel identity and relative time, and uses topology-separated context to exclude immediate spatial and temporal neighborhoods from visible input. Evaluation spans 30 downstream datasets covering clinical diagnosis, sleep staging, emotion recognition, motor imagery, event-related potentials, cognitive-state decoding, and language retrieval.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: EEG foundation models are large-scale architectures that learn universal representations from heterogeneous brain signal data using self-supervised methods such as masked prediction. Shortcut learning occurs when models exploit spurious patterns—like stable positional cues or local correlations—that solve training data but fail to generalize. NSP addresses this by making masked regions harder to predict through low-information paths, forcing the model to integrate distributed neural context.

## [SemNav Boosts Repository Issue Localization with LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31176) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced SemNav, a framework that combines deterministic retrieval with LLM agents to improve repository-level issue localization. On SWE-bench Lite and PLocBench, SemNav raised File Hit@10 from 68.33% to 82.67% with Gemma 4B and improved downstream issue resolution from 44.00% to 52.33%. Issue localization is a critical bottleneck in automated debugging and code maintenance, and SemNav's gains suggest that combining broad retrieval with evidence-guided LLM refinement can make AI coding agents substantially more effective. This could benefit developers using agentic tools for bug fixing and repository-level software engineering tasks. SemNav relies on three components: a Semantic Navigation Graph that uses a language server to resolve program relations on demand, Issue-conditioned Semantic Cards that provide compact source-grounded entity interpretations, and a persistent Candidate Workspace that records each candidate with its evidential basis. Semantic Cards reduced working-context load by 48.2% compared to full-source reading, and SemNav ranked first on all seven evidence-quality metrics on SWE-Explore.

**Background**: Repository-level issue localization is the task of identifying and ranking the files and functions relevant to resolving a reported software issue. LLM agents typically approach this iteratively by proposing candidate locations, inspecting code, and revising judgments as new evidence arrives, but existing environments offer limited support for this loop. SemNav addresses these limitations by seeding a broad candidate set through deterministic retrieval and then refining it with an LLM agent guided by a semantic navigation graph.

## [SPO: LLM Framework Discovers Adaptive LNS Operators via Stackelberg Game](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31179) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced Stackelberg Program Optimization (SPO), an LLM-based framework that automatically discovers adaptive executable destroy-repair programs for large neighborhood search (LNS). By modeling the asymmetric interaction between destroy and repair operators as a Stackelberg game, SPO outperforms strong baselines on the traveling salesperson problem (TSP) and the capacitated vehicle routing problem (CVRP), and generalizes to larger instances and benchmark sets. This work shows that LLMs can be used not just to generate code but to co-evolve interacting algorithm components, potentially reducing the manual effort of designing LNS operators for combinatorial optimization. If the approach generalizes, it could accelerate the development of adaptive solvers for logistics, routing, and scheduling applications. SPO conditions operator decisions on a compact LNS state so that state-dependent behavior emerges through program discovery, and it uses role-specific credits that treat destroy programs as leaders and repair programs as conditional followers. The coupled optimization combines LLM generator learning with population-based evolutionary search over programs, and behavioral analyses confirm state-dependent operator behavior and coupled destroy-repair improvement.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Large neighborhood search (LNS) is a metaheuristic that repeatedly destroys part of a solution and repairs it to find better solutions, and its performance depends heavily on the design of destroy and repair operators. Adaptive LNS (ALNS) dynamically selects among multiple operators, but the operators themselves are usually hand-crafted. A Stackelberg game is a leader-follower model in which one player commits to a strategy first and the other responds optimally, which matches the asymmetric dependency between destroy and repair roles.

## [Latent Variable Framework Corrects Bias in LLM Judges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31184) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.31184) proposes a unified latent variable framework that jointly models pairwise and ordinal comparison data while explicitly correcting for documented LLM judge biases such as position bias, verbosity bias, judge severity, and self-enhancement. The authors claim this approach recovers reliable model rankings from substantially fewer comparisons than current leaderboards require. Current LLM leaderboards compensate for judge bias by running ever more comparisons, which is statistically unsound and computationally wasteful. This work could make evaluation both more rigorous and more sustainable, directly affecting how leaderboards and benchmarks are built across the AI/ML ecosystem. The framework explicitly models confounders that no volume of additional data can eliminate, and fitting the model costs negligible compute relative to a single round of LLM inference. It is an arXiv preprint without peer review or community discussion yet, so empirical validation across diverse judges and tasks remains to be seen.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: LLM-as-a-judge has become the de facto standard for scalable subjective evaluation, where a language model scores or compares outputs instead of human annotators. Latent variable models are statistical models that relate observed data to unobserved variables, commonly used in psychometrics and item response theory to separate true ability from measurement artifacts. Documented judge biases include position bias (favoring the first or second option), verbosity bias (favoring longer answers), judge severity, and self-enhancement (favoring outputs from the same model family).

## [New 'Evolutionary Safety' Framework Targets Recursive Self-Improving AI Risks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31186) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.31186) introduces 'Evolutionary Safety,' a perspective for studying how safety properties change, persist, accumulate, and propagate in recursively self-improving AI systems. It characterizes recurring failure modes such as intent drift, error accumulation, experience contamination, safety-property erosion, evaluator drift, and risk propagation, and develops a taxonomy spanning persistent agent state, model state, evaluation and environmental feedback, computational substrate, and meta-level update mechanisms. As AI systems increasingly participate in their own improvement—through model training, experience accumulation, agent evolution, and automated AI development—maintaining safety becomes a moving-target problem that current static evaluation methods may not capture. This framework offers AI safety and alignment researchers a structured vocabulary and risk-discovery approach for a critical, underexplored area, and derives governance principles for modification, selection, authorization, provenance, and recovery. The paper examines how evolutionary risks can be discovered and evaluated across states, updates, trajectories, and lineages, and outlines open problems toward maintaining safety guarantees as systems become more persistent, adaptive, and recursively self-improving. Project resources and proposed evaluation systems are available at the linked project page, though the abstract is truncated and no empirical results are described in the provided content.

**Background**: Recursive self-improvement (RSI) is a hypothesized process in which an AI system rewrites its own code or improves its own capabilities, potentially leading to rapid capability gains; no attempt so far has shown signs of an intelligence explosion. AI alignment, a subfield of AI safety, aims to steer AI systems toward intended goals and prevent misalignment, reward hacking, and emergent harmful behaviors. This paper extends that discussion to the evolutionary dynamics of safety itself under persistent self-improvement.

## [Data Scaling Decomposed into Samples, Sources, and Spatial Coverage for Brain Microarchitecture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31201) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.31201) reframes data scaling as an allocation problem, separating unique sample count, source diversity, and spatial coverage, and tests this decomposition across 93 controlled pretraining runs of a contrastive model on 11.6 million spatially anchored image patches from 21 human brains. It finds that performance improves with more unique samples, broader spatial coverage, additional compute, and larger model capacity, but that distributing a fixed sample budget across one to 18 subjects yields no detectable improvement. This work challenges the common practice of representing training data by a single sample count, showing that for hierarchically and spatially structured data such as whole-brain histology, source diversity and spatial coverage are distinct axes that affect generalization differently. It has implications for how neuroscience and medical imaging researchers allocate scarce data collection and annotation budgets, and more broadly for representation learning on any spatially structured domain. The study uses a contrastive model that leverages spatial proximity for supervision, with a source defined as an individual brain and a sample as an image patch at a specific spatial location. A notable caveat is that representations generalize substantially better to subjects encountered during pretraining, indicating that inter-subject variation strongly affects generalization even though adding more subjects at a fixed sample budget provides no benefit.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Scaling studies in machine learning typically summarize training data with a single count of samples, implicitly assuming samples are interchangeable. In whole-brain histology, however, data is hierarchically and spatially structured: patches come from individual brains and occupy specific locations within a larger anatomical space. Contrastive learning is a self-supervised approach that trains representations by pulling similar (positive) pairs together and pushing dissimilar (negative) pairs apart; here, spatial proximity defines which patches are treated as similar.

## [Influence Estimators Disagree Due to Counterfactual Specification Mismatch](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31214) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.31214) argues that disagreements among influence estimators in data attribution stem primarily from specification mismatch—differences in the counterfactual estimand—rather than approximation error. The authors formalize influence as a counterfactual estimand, organize representative estimators by their implied specifications, and derive a local decomposition linking behavior signals, training signals, and counterfactual parameter responses. This reframing could change how practitioners interpret and compare data attribution methods, since choosing a behavior surrogate (e.g., query loss, logit, margin) can produce different rankings even with exact estimation. It suggests specification analysis should be a necessary first step in data debugging, valuation, and LLM attribution workflows. Controlled experiments show exact estimands under different specifications can induce different rankings, while approximation error grows as perturbations move farther from their linearization points. Experiments on noisy label detection and LLM attribution demonstrate that behavior-aligned specifications can identify target-specific training examples obscured by default loss-based or similarity-based specifications.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Data attribution aims to trace a model's outputs back to the training examples responsible, often using influence functions that estimate how up-weighting or removing a training point would change model behavior. In statistics, an estimand is the precise quantity being estimated, and a counterfactual estimand defines that quantity under a hypothetical intervention. Existing influence estimators often yield incompatible rankings, which are typically blamed on approximation error from linearization or sampling.

Researchers introduced DIAL, a unified framework that combines abundant LLM comparisons with limited human comparisons to separate judge-specific position effects, learn shared structure in position-debiased LLM preferences, and adaptively calibrate that structure toward human preference targets. The paper provides theoretical analysis of identification, adaptive estimation, and uncertainty quantification, plus empirical validation on three human-preference benchmarks and a real-data study collecting over 410K judgments from 21 LLM judges in both display orders. LLM-as-a-judge is increasingly used for scalable evaluation, but position bias and systematic divergence from human preferences undermine its reliability. DIAL offers a principled way to correct these issues with limited human labels, which could improve the trustworthiness of automated evaluation in AI alignment and model development. The framework separates latent LLM preferences, position effects, and human calibration, and uses adaptive estimation to balance LLM anchoring against limited human evidence. The real-data study includes 410K judgments from 21 LLM judges in both display orders, providing a resource for studying LLM-judge bias and heterogeneity.

## [MA-WAM: Multi-Agent World-Action Model for Test-Time Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31281) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced MA-WAM, a test-time planning framework that lets a frozen multi-agent flow policy evaluate candidate joint actions by predicting their consequences through cross-agent dependencies. Across 30 offline MARL settings on MAMuJoCo, SMAC, and MPE, MA-WAM achieves mean relative gains of 22.0% over direct execution and 25.6% over uniform action selection, adding only 12.1 ms (2.5% of generation-and-scoring time) on an A100 GPU. This is claimed to be the first test-time world-model planner for multi-agent flow policies, addressing a key limitation of such policies in cooperative settings where each agent's action affects others' observations and responses. It could improve decision quality in time-sensitive, resource-constrained multi-agent applications without retraining the underlying policy. A naive extension that applies a single-agent world model to each agent's action step by step fails to capture dependencies among simultaneous actions, which MA-WAM explicitly models to score joint-action candidates efficiently. The abstract is truncated and no community discussion is available, so broader validation remains to be seen.

**Background**: Multi-agent cooperative tasks require agents to execute joint actions simultaneously, and each agent's action affects both the observations and responses of the others, so a world model is needed to predict the team return resulting from joint actions. Multi-agent flow policies generate actions through iterative sampling, which can be slow, and test-time planning uses a learned model to evaluate candidate futures at inference without updating policy weights. MA-WAM combines these ideas by rolling out and scoring fixed-length joint-action sequences proposed by a frozen policy.

<a id="item-105"></a>
## [Accuracy-Energy Trade-offs in Local Information Extraction Pipelines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31341) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.31341) benchmarks small (≤8B parameter) text-only and vision-language models for on-premise information extraction, evaluating accuracy and energy across input representation, model family, and inference configuration. It finds that batching is the dominant energy lever, cutting energy per page by 38–85% at no accuracy cost, while FP8 quantization saves 27–32% only when requests are served one at a time. The results give concrete, actionable guidance for organizations that must process privacy-sensitive documents on-premise without cloud services, showing that the best pipeline depends on document layout rather than a single universal choice. This matters as more enterprises adopt small local models for compliance reasons and need to balance accuracy against energy costs. Preprocessing dominates remaining energy use: neural OCR costs 17× more energy per page than classical OCR and never reaches the Pareto frontier. The winning input representation flips with document type — vision-language models win on layout-rich VRDU forms, while small text-only models with a cheap parser win on near-plain-text Kleister-NDA contracts, being both more accurate and cheaper than any vision-language configuration.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Information extraction (also called key information extraction) is the task of pulling structured fields out of documents such as contracts and forms, and it has increasingly shifted from rule-based heuristics to large language models and vision-language models. Vision-language models can read page images directly, while text-only models require a parsing or OCR step to convert documents into text first. FP8 quantization stores model weights and activations in 8-bit floating point to reduce memory and compute, and batching means processing multiple requests together to amortize overhead.

## [Mutable Transcripts: Editable Chat History to Curb Context Pollution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31354) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.31354) introduces "mutable transcripts," an interaction paradigm that lets users revise prior conversation turns via natural-language edit requests, so the transcript is updated rather than appended. The authors built a working prototype (open-sourced as ReChat) and validated it with a controlled user study (n=17) plus transcript analysis of representative scenarios. Context pollution—where outdated or irrelevant turns keep influencing later LLM responses—is a real pain point in long chats, and this work reframes the transcript from a passive log into an editable representation of conversational state. If adopted, it could change how chat interfaces and agent memory systems are designed, reducing the need to restart conversations and helping models track evolving user intent. Participants significantly preferred mutable transcripts over standard chat on clarity, confidence, and ease of use, and reported less intent to restart conversations; transcript analysis showed shorter conversations and elimination of obsolete retained context. The authors note the paradigm is not primarily aimed at improving immediate task success in short interactions, but at preserving conversational progress while reducing accumulated context burden and transcript growth over iterative correction cycles.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: LLM chat systems typically treat conversation history as an immutable sequence of turns that forms the model's working context, and this context is fed back into the model on every subsequent request. Because user intent evolves through corrections and shifting constraints, stale turns can persist and "pollute" later responses, a problem known as context pollution. Existing approaches to managing conversation state—such as summarization, truncation, or memory modules—usually operate on the history without letting the user directly edit it.

## [LOHA and Anchored Context Distillation Compress Software Agent Histories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31430) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.31430) proposes Latent Observations, Hard Actions (LOHA), a context layout that compresses older tool observations into soft tokens while keeping the agent's own turns and the last K observations in plain text, combined with Anchored Context Distillation (ACD), a training method that teaches the agent to read the latent view while anchoring its behavior to the base model on plain-text inputs. On SWE-bench Verified, K=3 cuts context per call by 43% for Qwen3-4B and 57% for SWE-Master-4B-RL, with resolve rates of 12.1% and 21.8% versus 14.5% and 27.5% for the uncompressed baselines. Tool observations dominate the context of software-engineering agents, so long interaction histories are expensive to maintain and degrade performance; this work targets the real cost-versus-behavior tradeoff that every production coding agent faces. If validated, the approach could let agents run longer tasks within fixed token budgets and serve more concurrent instances per GPU. Compression is not free: at K=3 resolve rates drop relative to uncompressed baselines, and a recency sweep shows larger windows (K=8) recover performance to 14.4% and 23.0% while compressing less. Under a 32K-token limit, Qwen3 with K=3 resolves 21.1% of a 199-instance subset versus 11.1% for the same adapted agent using full text, and achieves 1.9x that full-text agent's instance throughput in concurrent single-GPU serving.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Software-engineering agents such as SWE-bench-style systems repeatedly call tools (shell, tests, file readers) and append the outputs to their context, so histories grow quickly and become costly. Prior context-compression methods either discard information that later actions need, or adapt the agent to soft-token representations in ways that change its original behavior. LOHA addresses this by separating compressed history from text that must be referenced exactly, while ACD uses knowledge distillation to let the model read the latent view without drifting from its base behavior.

<a id="item-108"></a>
## [UQ-LOB Adds Calibrated Uncertainty to Limit Order Book Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31491) ⭐️ 7.0/10

UQ-LOB introduces a lightweight, encoder-agnostic uncertainty quantification module that attaches to any pretrained limit order book (LOB) encoder and, inspired by attentive neural processes, conditions each forecast on a context set of recently completed windows. Tested on 5.2 billion LOB events across seven cryptocurrency assets at 5, 10, and 15-second horizons, its regression variant achieves near-nominal 68% interval coverage, and filtering to the most confident 10% of predictions raises directional macro F1 by 0.11-0.15 (regression) and 0.05-0.11 (classification). Most deep LOB forecasters are point predictors that never indicate which forecasts can be trusted, which is a serious limitation for risk-aware algorithmic trading. By exposing a scalar confidence score that supports selective prediction, UQ-LOB could let trading systems abstain on low-confidence signals and act only on high-confidence ones, improving reliability without retraining the underlying encoder. The module offers two variants: UQ-regression outputs a calibrated Gaussian over future tick displacement, while UQ-classification outputs a categorical distribution over down/up/stationary, both exposing a scalar confidence (predicted signal-to-noise ratio or class probability). On large, economically meaningful moves, the tightest confidence tier reaches directional F1 of 0.88 (down) and 0.83 (up) at the 5-second horizon, though results are demonstrated only on cryptocurrency assets rather than traditional equities or futures.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: A limit order book (LOB) is a digital record of all outstanding buy and sell orders for a financial instrument, and forecasting short-horizon mid-price movements from it is central to algorithmic trading. Deep learning models have increasingly been applied to LOB forecasting, but they typically output only a direction or displacement without any measure of confidence. Uncertainty quantification (UQ) in deep learning aims to estimate how reliable a model's predictions are, and attentive neural processes are a meta-learning approach that uses attention to condition predictions on observed context while producing calibrated uncertainty.

## [Flow Matching Unifies Neural Representational Dissimilarity Metrics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31544) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.31544) shows that many commonly used neural representational dissimilarity metrics can be unified as Jeffreys divergences under different velocity constraints within a flow matching framework. The authors demonstrate that flow matching improves estimation for complex distributions and continuous variables, and enables principled design of new distance metrics. This unification bridges deep generative models and computational neuroscience, offering a single theoretical lens for comparing neural codes across stimuli, brain areas, tasks, and models. It could lead to more robust and interpretable metrics for both AI/ML researchers and neuroscientists studying representational similarity. The framework recasts distances as Jeffreys divergences arising from different velocity constraints in flow matching, and the authors highlight advantages for complicated distributions and continuous variables. The work is a specialized arXiv preprint without accompanying community discussion, so broader validation is still pending.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Flow matching is a technique in deep generative models that learns a continuous transformation (flow) mapping a simple distribution to a complex data distribution, often by matching velocity fields. Jeffreys divergence is a symmetric statistical measure of difference between two probability distributions, related to the Kullback-Leibler divergence. Neural representational dissimilarity quantifies how neural response distributions differ, typically via distance metrics such as Euclidean or Mahalanobis distances, and is central to comparing neural codes.

## [Steiner's Taxonomy Reveals Limits of Multi-Agent LLM Scaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31563) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.31563) introduces Steiner's taxonomy of group tasks as a framework for analyzing multi-agent LLM scaling, testing 13 open-weight models with teams of up to 30 agents. It finds that on disjunctive tasks the probability of at least one correct answer grows by 5-20 points with team size, but plurality voting captures almost none of this gain, while on compensatory tasks like Fermi estimation scaling yields little benefit because item-level biases account for about 87% of squared error. This work offers a theoretical lens for why simply adding more LLM agents does not reliably improve performance, showing that task structure and the aggregation mechanism jointly determine scaling outcomes. It gives practitioners concrete guidance that naive plurality voting is a poor fit for disjunctive tasks, which could reshape how multi-agent systems are designed. The analysis models independently sampled agents as conditionally independent given the item, yielding large-team limits where plurality voting converges to the model's modal answer and averaging converges to its item-level bias. Multi-round revision substantially raises accuracy, but the gain with one peer is nearly the same as with 29, and combining model families helps on Fermi estimation yet does not surpass the strongest member on disjunctive tasks.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Steiner's taxonomy, from his book Group Processes and Productivity, classifies group tasks by how member contributions combine, and is used to explain coordination losses in groups. Disjunctive tasks are completed when a single best solution is adopted, so a larger team mainly raises the chance that someone finds the right answer, whereas compensatory tasks can be completed by mathematically averaging members' solutions. Multi-agent LLM systems, where several language-model instances collaborate on a query, are often assumed to improve with team size, and this paper tests that assumption against these task types.

## [Self-Supervised Confidence Training Cuts LLM Reasoning Tokens by 25%](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31619) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.31619) shows that fine-tuning reasoning models to predict their own confidence at intermediate reasoning steps, using only 600 training problems, reduces generated tokens by up to 25% at matched accuracy across Gemma, Qwen, Nemotron, and GPT-OSS models. Crucially, the training loss contains no objective for reasoning length, efficiency, or stopping, and inference uses the standard generation procedure with no early-stopping mechanism. This suggests efficient reasoning can emerge as a downstream consequence of learning metacognitive signals, rather than being directly optimized, offering a cheaper and simpler alternative to inference-time early stopping or reinforcement learning with length penalties. If it generalizes, it could substantially lower the inference cost of long chain-of-thought reasoning models used in math, science, and coding. The method, sometimes called ConfSFT, uses confidence only as a training target and requires just 600 training problems; analysis of reasoning episodes shows confidence supervision largely preserves the base models' high-level reasoning composition rather than selectively suppressing particular behaviors. The abstract is truncated, so full details on the self-supervised confidence labeling procedure and evaluation setup are not yet available.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Reasoning models such as those trained with long chain-of-thought generate very long traces, making inference computationally expensive. Existing efficiency approaches typically rely on inference-time early stopping or on explicitly encouraging shorter reasoning during training, for example via reinforcement learning with length penalties, which can distort reasoning behavior. This paper instead explores confidence estimation as a self-supervised training signal, connecting to broader work on metacognition and efficient LLM inference.

## [ENAS: GPU-Free Hardware-Aware NAS for TinyML on Microcontrollers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30272) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers released ENAS, a hardware-aware Neural Architecture Search framework that runs without GPU acceleration, combining a static feasibility check, a cell-based search space, and a three-stage hybrid search strategy (random → top-K → mutation) with persistent cross-run caching. Evaluated on Visual Wake Words and Melanoma Cancer benchmarks across eight microcontrollers (20 KB to 1 MB SRAM) and nine input resolutions, ENAS achieves mean search-time speedups of 2.41x and 1.70x respectively versus NanoNAS, while reaching 79.4% test accuracy on an STM32H743 microcontroller, 2.6 percentage points above a greedy CPU-only baseline. Most NAS frameworks depend on GPU clusters, which excludes developers working in resource-constrained environments; by removing that requirement, ENAS makes hardware-aware architecture search accessible to embedded and edge-AI practitioners. Its focus on peak activation RAM — the binding constraint for microcontroller deployment — and its open-source release could accelerate TinyML adoption in IoT, wearables, and real-time systems. The search space supports standard, depthwise-separable, and bottleneck blocks with optional skip connections, and the framework is validated across memory footprints from 20 KB to 1 MB SRAM and nine image resolutions. ENAS-selected models use substantially lower peak activation RAM than NanoNAS at matched accuracy, though the paper reports results only on two TinyML benchmarks and the speedups are relative to a single recent baseline.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Neural Architecture Search (NAS) automates the design of neural networks by defining a search space, a search strategy, and a performance estimation method, often producing models that rival hand-designed architectures. TinyML targets deploying models on microcontrollers and ultra-low-power embedded devices with only kilobytes of RAM and milliwatt power budgets. Depthwise-separable convolutions, popularized by MobileNet, split a standard convolution into a depthwise and a pointwise step to cut computation and parameters, making them a common building block for such constrained models.

## [Sparse Neurons in Frozen BERT Drive AI-Text Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30287) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.30287) applies L1-to-L2 sparse probing to all 9,216 CLS hidden-state dimensions of frozen BERT-base-uncased, recovering a stable set of under 1% of neurons per generator on the RAID benchmark across six generators. Bidirectional activation patching confirms these neurons are causally relevant, flipping predictions an order of magnitude more often than size-matched random sets. This work bridges mechanistic interpretability and AI-text detection, showing that a detector can operate on a small fixed subspace without re-identifying neurons per generator, which could lead to more robust and transferable detection systems. It also offers evidence that instruction-tuned models leave a distinct layer-12 footprint in BERT, linking post-training alignment to detectable internal representations. Mean-ablating the identified neurons leaves accuracy largely intact, indicating the detection signal is redundantly distributed rather than concentrated. Cross-generator analysis reveals a bipartite structure: instruction-tuned generators concentrate 30-36% of stable neurons in BERT's final layer, while base generators fall below 14%, and leave-one-family-out evaluation shows the selected neurons retain 86-94% of the full-feature ceiling on unseen generator families.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: AI-generated text detectors often achieve high accuracy on benchmarks like RAID, but the internal representations driving their predictions are poorly understood. Sparse probing trains k-sparse linear classifiers on internal activations to identify which neurons encode specific features, while activation patching (causal tracing) swaps internal activations to test whether a component is causally responsible for a prediction. BERT-base-uncased is a widely used transformer encoder with 12 layers and 768 hidden dimensions, yielding 9,216 CLS hidden-state dimensions.

## [Cartograph: Federated MCP Tool Discovery for AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30293) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.30293) introduces Cartograph, a federated MCP proxy that reduces agent-visible tool discovery from O(n) catalog traversal to O(k) progressive disclosure using operator-attested capability cards, a three-layer confusable-cluster analysis called Rift, and two-stage retrieval that ranks servers before tools. On a 22-server, 374-tool deployment it exposes only three proxy tools instead of 374 definitions, achieving R@5 of 0.816 on a 49-query benchmark versus 0.592 for a Jaccard keyword baseline. As MCP catalogs grow, loading every tool definition into an agent's context becomes prohibitively expensive, so Cartograph's approach could make large federated tool ecosystems practical for AI agents. It also addresses provenance and trust by signing capability cards under the deploying operator's control rather than relying on publisher-written descriptions. A measured top-5 discovery exchange consumes 475 tokens instead of 42,450 under full-catalog accounting, and gateway measurements over ten trials add only 5ms mean latency (0.8%) versus direct stdio MCP calls. Rift identified 49 confusable clusters including four HIGH-risk clusters in bootstrap-generated cards, and an exploratory comparison of 119 LLM-generated descriptions removed the zero-distance cluster but showed that mixing card-generation regimes can reduce R@5.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: The Model Context Protocol (MCP) is an open standard that lets AI applications connect to external data sources and tools, but agents typically must load tool definitions to know what is available. Cartograph is a proxy layer that sits between agents and MCP servers, surfacing only a small ranked subset of tools per query. It is described as complementary to code-execution approaches because it controls which tool descriptions are surfaced and records the provenance of descriptions used for ranking.

The synthetic data pipeline transforms single-turn prompts into realistic multi-turn conversations, enabling systematic pre-launch evaluation, while the self-improvement loop combines variance-based contrastive optimization with iterative refinement through a coding agent to automatically identify and fix planning and tool-use errors. Cold-start settings are challenging because no real multi-turn user interactions exist yet to train or evaluate the agent, and synthetic data generation with large language models has emerged as a way to create realistic, diverse conversations for such scenarios. ## [EuroPLoP 2026 Focus Group: What Stays Human in AI-Era Architecture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30334) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A focus group at EuroPLoP 2026 with 22 industry and academic participants found broad consensus that architectural decision-making, accountability, and the authoring of architectural guardrails remain fundamentally human tasks. The report introduces 'harness engineering' as a new discipline for building the system that governs AI-assisted system creation, and identifies 'criticality' (uncertainty combined with cost of change) as the universal criterion for calibrating human oversight. As AI development agents take on more software architecture work, this practitioner-informed report gives the software engineering community an early vocabulary for dividing labor between humans and AI. The concepts of harness engineering and cognitive debt could shape how organizations design governance, validation, and education around AI-assisted development. The study highlights 'cognitive debt' — the progressive erosion of human understanding of a system when AI-assisted decisions are accepted without full intellectual engagement — as a key concern, and describes harness engineering as comprising validation mechanisms, knowledge layers, and company-specific standards. The findings are qualitative, drawn from a single focus group of 22 participants, so they represent practitioner consensus rather than empirically validated results.

AI development agents are tools that can autonomously or semi-autonomously perform software engineering tasks such as writing code and proposing designs. Architectural guardrails are constraints and standards that keep a codebase aligned with approved decisions and frameworks, while architectural decision-making concerns the high-level structural choices that shape a system.

## [Sola Security Brain Outperforms Claude Code on Cloud-Security Investigations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30345) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper evaluates the Sola Security Brain, a purpose-built security intelligence layer, against Claude Code operating the same live AWS environment via a read-only CLI across 28 cloud-security investigation tasks. The Sola Security Brain achieved 0.693 coverage versus Claude Code's 0.387, a 79.2% relative gain, leading on 25 of 28 tasks while using 17.7x less reasoning cost per task. This finding suggests that general-purpose coding agents, despite their growing capabilities, may not be sufficient for enterprise security investigations that require complete population-level answers rather than partial object-level results. It provides evidence that purpose-built context layers with deterministic relational substrates still deliver substantial accuracy and cost advantages, which could influence how enterprises architect agentic security tooling. The evaluation used a blinded, tier-weighted, grounding-gated relative recall scoring method averaged over three independent grading draws, with the coverage gap varying by only ±0.018. The paper also identifies a failure pattern called 'sample-and-generalise,' where the live agent enumerates a fraction of a large population, asserts an unhedged universal negative, and discloses the sample size only in metadata—such as reporting no bucket policies exist after checking 4 of roughly 5,000 buckets in an account where 65 buckets carry wildcard-principal read grants.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Cloud-security investigation often involves 'population tasks'—questions like which identities can read a data store or how many resources fail a control—that must be resolved against a complete inventory rather than a single named object. General-purpose coding agents such as Claude Code can now be given read-only cloud credentials to investigate directly, raising the question of what a specialized security context layer still contributes. Sola Security Brain is a security intelligence layer whose relational substrate is resolved offline and whose security logic is evaluated against it at query time.

## [Adaptive multi-resolution Gaussian processes enable scalable exact inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30348) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces an adaptive multi-resolution Gaussian process framework that constructs naturally data-sparse covariance matrices by anchoring multi-resolution basis functions directly to samples, eliminating auxiliary points. It achieves exact inference with O(n log^2 n) training cost and O(log^d n) prediction cost via sparse Cholesky inversion. This work addresses the long-standing scalability bottleneck in Gaussian process regression, where exact inference typically scales cubically with data size. By achieving exact inference at near-linear training cost, it could make high-fidelity probabilistic models practical for large datasets in machine learning and spatial statistics. The sparsity is guaranteed by shrinking the support domains of the multi-resolution basis functions, which limits matrix block sizes. An augmented basis function is also constructed to improve predictive uncertainties, and theoretical analysis plus numerical experiments support the claimed complexity.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Gaussian processes are a cornerstone of probabilistic machine learning, defining distributions over functions where any finite set of points follows a multivariate normal distribution. Exact inference requires inverting the covariance matrix, which scales cubically with the number of data points and becomes impractical beyond a few thousand samples. Existing scalable approaches often rely on approximations or auxiliary inducing points, trading fidelity for speed.

## [Unfaithful LLM Providers Can Exploit Self-Consistency Billing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30352) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.30352) shows that an unfaithful LLM provider can exploit self-consistency's pay-per-path billing model by generating and strategically reordering extra reasoning paths so that every path appears necessary to reach the majority vote, inflating costs while evading auditor detection. Experiments across Llama and Qwen instruct models and DeepSeek-R1-distilled reasoning models on math, science, and QA benchmarks show the extra-path distribution is heavy-tailed and substantial overcharging capacity remains even under the best audit keeping false positives below α = 0.1. This exposes a novel economic vulnerability in a widely used reasoning technique, showing that financial incentives in pay-per-path API pricing can be weaponized against users without triggering standard detection. It matters for AI safety, API pricing transparency, and any developer or enterprise relying on self-consistency for high-stakes reasoning tasks. The attack uses a simple, efficient algorithm that generates and reorders additional reasoning paths so each appears necessary for the majority vote, and the resulting distribution of extra paths is heavy-tailed. Even under the best possible audit designed to keep the false-positive rate below α = 0.1, substantial overcharging capacity remains, and validation spans multiple model families and benchmark domains.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Self-consistency is a popular test-time inference technique that improves LLM reasoning by sampling multiple diverse reasoning paths and selecting the final answer via majority vote, as introduced in the chain-of-thought line of work (arXiv:2203.11171). Because providers typically charge users in proportion to the number of reasoning paths generated, they have a direct financial incentive to inflate path counts, which this paper shows can be exploited covertly.

## [DanLing NestedTensor: Composable Multi-Ragged Tensors for Deep Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30379) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.30379) introduces DanLing NestedTensor, a PyTorch tensor abstraction that makes multi-ragged structure a native property of the tensor itself, carrying tensor-backed partitions and logical dimension order through broadcasting, feature transforms, reductions, autograd, and both eager and compiled execution. On an A100, it reports geometric-mean speedups over same-mode padding of 2.74x eager and 3.39x compiled across four BERT scales, 1.97x eager across four FCN backbones, and 2.40-4.32x on a four-block Pairformer-style workload, with peak allocation dropping from 38.08 to 5.41 GiB on a high-variation batch. Padding waste is a pervasive inefficiency in deep learning, and the cost multiplies across multiple varying axes, such as the BN_max^2 positions an explicit pair state allocates instead of the sum of N_i^2. By making raggedness a first-class tensor property that composes without offset bookkeeping at call sites, this work could let model code built from supported operators run variable-size computation efficiently in both eager and compiled PyTorch, potentially benefiting transformer and structured-attention workloads. The abstraction lets packed values carry tensor-backed partitions and logical dimension order, so broadcasting creates ragged axes, feature transformations retain them, and reductions consume them, with the same representation flowing through autograd and compiled execution. The reported results are on an A100 and the code is promised to be released publicly upon publication, so the claims have not yet been independently reproduced.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Deep learning models often process variable-size inputs, and the standard approach is dense batching, which allocates a shared padded envelope and spends computation on padding. Sequence packing concatenates shorter sequences into one longer buffer to avoid padding memory, but a flat packed buffer no longer exposes the logical axes and sample boundaries needed to compose operations. PyTorch has a prototype NestedTensor feature for nested data, but it is a prototype with incomplete operator coverage, and DanLing NestedTensor aims to make multi-ragged structure composable through the tensor interface itself.

A new arXiv paper (arXiv:2609.30402) presents a large-scale empirical study of multimodal misinformation detection, running over 3,375 experiments across three benchmark datasets and a wide range of pre-trained vision and language backbones. The study systematically compares design choices and conducts targeted robustness analyses to answer four key research questions about which choices help, when they fail silently, and what most strongly shapes model behavior. Multimodal misinformation that pairs a textual claim with a seemingly supporting image is increasingly convincing, and this study offers practical, evidence-based guidance for researchers and practitioners building detectors. By identifying which design choices matter and when they fail silently, it can help the community avoid unreliable pipelines and build more dependable detection systems. The study spans three benchmark datasets and a broad range of pre-trained vision and language backbones, totaling more than 3,375 experiments, and combines systematic comparisons with targeted robustness analyses. Its main contribution is distilled practical guidance rather than a new model or a state-of-the-art result, and it explicitly frames its findings around four research questions.

## [CLUE Framework Actively Resolves Contextual Uncertainty in Underspecified Robot Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30428) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced CLUE (Closed-Loop contextual Uncertainty rEsolution), a framework that enables robots to actively resolve contextual uncertainty for underspecified natural language tasks by using an LLM-derived policy to hypothesize task-relevant concepts and an online-built language-embedded map to ground those hypotheses into actions. Deployed on a Boston Dynamics Spot across three real indoor and outdoor environments and 15 tasks, CLUE achieved a success rate within 7 percentage points of an oracle policy and outperformed an LLM-enabled planner without closed-loop feedback by a 4x margin. Most language-conditioned robot policies assume goals are well-specified and that task-relevant information is provided upfront via a prior map, which breaks down in unfamiliar environments. CLUE shows that closed-loop interaction and online language-embedded mapping can substantially improve real-world task success, pointing toward more autonomous robots that can operate in unstructured settings. Supporting experiments show that simply building and then querying a language-enriched map is insufficient for complex contextual planning, achieving roughly one third of CLUE's success rate while consuming over 10x more VLM tokens. The system was evaluated on tasks requiring object disambiguation, functional inference, and occlusion reasoning, and additional information is available at the project page.

**Background**: Language-conditioned robotics refers to systems where robots interpret free-form language inputs to parameterize perception, planning, and action. Language-embedded maps use embeddings from vision-language models to represent semantics in robotic maps, enabling open-vocabulary scene understanding beyond traditional fixed labels. Foundation models such as LLMs and VLMs provide high-level reasoning and grounding capabilities that increasingly serve as the 'brain' for robot policies.

## [ContextXR: A Benchmarking Framework for Context-aware XR Interfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30466) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces ContextXR, a benchmarking framework that represents an XR application as a connected graph of functional facets, each a semantically coherent group of capabilities supporting a shared user intent. It also presents MineXR++, a dataset augmenting prior XR interface data with facet-level annotations, and defines three canonical tasks: context factor analysis, initial facet suggestion, and next facet suggestion. Context-aware XR interfaces are difficult to evaluate because current prototyping and user-study workflows offer no systematic, repeatable way to compare adaptation methods across users and scenarios. ContextXR fills this gap by enabling reproducible, quantitative comparison of suggestion methods, which could shape how XR and HCI researchers design and validate context-aware functionality in the future. The evaluation protocol scores suggestion methods using a simulated interaction metric based on the navigation and search cost of reaching the desired functionality. The authors benchmark global popularity, relational retrieval, and LLM-based methods, demonstrating that ContextXR supports systematic and reproducible evaluation.

**Background**: Extended Reality (XR) covers augmented, virtual, and mixed reality systems, where users switch between contexts and expect the right functionality to appear at the right time and place with minimal manual reconfiguration. Benchmarking frameworks provide standardized datasets, tasks, and metrics so that competing methods can be compared fairly, a practice common in machine learning but less established for context-aware interface design. Functional facets are the framework's core abstraction: semantically coherent groups of related capabilities that together serve a shared user intent.

## [CARGO: Context-Aware Retrieval-Gated Evaluation for Agentic AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30471) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.30471) introduces CARGO, a context-aware, retrieval-gated evaluation framework for production agentic AI that treats retrieved references as procedural exemplars rather than literal targets, grounding factual judgments in the live instance's observed context. The authors also release CARGO-Bench, a 246-item perturbation-based diagnostic suite with ground truth by construction, on which CARGO eliminates false penalties (0/50) while retaining near-complete contradiction recall (50/50 and 49/50) and raising the discrimination index to 0.58. Reference-based LLM-as-a-judge evaluation is widely used to assess deployed agents, but it systematically penalizes correct answers when the closest reference applies the right procedure to a different entity — a failure mode the authors call reference-instance divergence (RID). CARGO offers a practical fix for teams running agents over dynamic entities such as support cases, assets, and accounts, where literal reference matching produces misleading quality signals and false hallucination flags. CARGO assigns each claim a three-way status (supported, contradicted, unverifiable) and penalizes only contradictions, gating evaluation by retrieval confidence so that production evaluation becomes a selective prediction problem. The paper candidly reports a limitation: the leniency that protects entity values suppresses detection of procedural corruptions (only 20% recall), and neither a post-hoc fix nor an LLM-as-annotator study with written guidelines closes that gap.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: LLM-as-a-judge is a common technique in which a large language model evaluates the outputs of another language-based system; reference-based variants compare the candidate answer against a gold reference. In production agentic systems operating over dynamic entities, the nearest available reference often applies the correct procedure to a different entity, so a literal judge treats differing identifiers, dates, and statuses as errors or hallucinations. Retrieval-gated evaluation borrows from selective prediction, where a system may abstain from judging when retrieval confidence is too low.

## [Persona Set Diversification Boosts LLM Creative Output Diversity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30492) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.30492) formulates persona diversification as a set-level conditioning problem and introduces four methods spanning coverage and dispersion subset selection, uniform-coverage sampling, and evolutionary persona generation. On the Alternative Uses Task, evolutionary persona generation increased response diversity by 78.8%, originality by 26.1%, flexibility by 49.5%, and holistic creativity by 13.9% over task-only prompting while maintaining 98.5% validity. This work shows that persona-set geometry is a task-agnostic mechanism for eliciting divergent LLM outputs, offering a reusable complement to prompt optimization that could help mitigate groupthink and homogeneity in AI-generated ideas. It has implications for creativity research, brainstorming tools, and any application where diverse perspectives from language models are valuable. The paper studies two orthogonal design choices—selecting versus generating personas, and space-filling versus frontier-seeking diversity—and finds that evolutionary personas compose with creativity-optimized prompting, further increasing response diversity by 18.6% and creativity by 6.3%. Evaluations span the Alternative Uses Task, Infinity-Chat, and the Divergent Association Task, though as a preprint the results have not yet undergone peer review.

The Divergent Association Task, published in 2021, measures verbal creativity by asking participants to name ten nouns that are as semantically different from each other as possible. Persona-based prompting conditions a language model on a described character or perspective, and this paper extends that idea from single personas to carefully diversified sets of personas.

## [Softmax Attention Shown to Implement Policy Mirror Descent as a Controller](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30500) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.30500) constructs a fixed causal-softmax actor–environment–one-step-critic protocol that implements negative-entropy policy mirror descent (PMD) as a repeated controller rather than a one-step algebraic identity. In a preregistered five-run S=4 test, separately trained pre-LN Transformers with an exact one-step critic reached a median returned-policy loss of 1.052× the Exact PMD oracle and retained the criterion across four no-retraining shifts. This work bridges Transformer attention theory and reinforcement learning optimization, suggesting that the softmax attention operation is not merely a heuristic but can provably realize a principled policy-improvement rule. If the construction generalizes, it could inform how Transformer-based controllers are designed and theoretically analyzed, affecting researchers in RL, control, and foundation-model interpretability. The construction requires a finite-logit/full-support domain, an external tokenization and sampling boundary, and mean-zero LayerNorm carrier conditions for the normalized compilation. At S=8, replacing the exact critic with a learned critic raises median T=20 loss to 0.0225 but still leaves Liang–Lai and Algorithm Distillation adaptations 20.2–24.2× higher-loss; the authors note this is a one-sided sampled-critic bound because PolicyAttention consumes 144 generative transitions per round versus 20 on-policy transitions for the adaptations, so the strict 20-transition comparison remains open.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Policy mirror descent (PMD) is a unifying framework in reinforcement learning that generalizes policy gradient methods by linking them to mirror descent, a first-order optimization technique; its negative-entropy variant has the statewise update softmax(log π + ηQ). Causal softmax attention is the masked attention mechanism used in decoder-style Transformers, where each position attends only to earlier positions, and pre-LN Transformers apply layer normalization before each sublayer, a convention now common in modern architectures. This paper asks whether such attention can implement PMD as a repeated closed-loop controller, not just as a single algebraic step.

## [Inquesto Score: A Reliability Protocol for Voice Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30514) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.30514) introduces Inquesto Score (IS), a protocol that measures voice-agent reliability as the percentage of calls in a fixed, versioned evaluation population that achieve the caller's goal without a functional failure or worse. Inquesto Score v0.1 evaluates 30 scenarios, three acoustic conditions, four speaker groups, and 306 calls per agent across 13 configurations of a reference voice-agent system, and the authors release the protocol, reference implementation, and evaluation records. Voice agents are increasingly deployed in workflows where failed interactions can affect transactions, access, and other consequential outcomes, yet the field has lacked a reproducible and interpretable way to compare reliability across systems. By defining explicit failure events and severity levels rather than blending heterogeneous metrics, Inquesto Score could become a shared benchmark that shapes how teams validate and deploy voice agents. Timing failures such as talk-over and delayed responses are measured directly from audio, while semantic and state-dependent failures are judged using scenario predicates, tool traces, and a pinned open-model judge. Diagnostic views of behavior, acoustic robustness, identity handling, and speaker groups accompany the score but are deliberately not combined into it, and the authors stress that reliable measurement requires evidence beyond transcripts and validation of the evaluators themselves.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Voice agents are AI systems that handle spoken conversations end-to-end, typically combining automatic speech recognition (ASR), a language model, and text-to-speech synthesis, and they are often evaluated with metrics like word error rate that only capture transcription fidelity. Word error rate says little about whether a caller actually achieved their goal, so recent work has argued that reliability should be tracked from intent capture through action. Inquesto Score fits this trend by treating the deployed pipeline as the unit of evaluation and grounding outcomes in audio, tool traces, and scenario-specific predicates.

A new arXiv paper audits latent-space failure monitors for two autonomous driving tasks: online vectorized map generation with LaneSegNet and end-to-end planning with VAD. It finds that a supervised latent probe reaches AUROC 0.780 for high Chamfer error on LaneSegNet and 0.868 for mean-ADE failure on VAD, but simple output-based monitors reach 0.825 and 0.924 respectively, with no statistically resolved improvement from adding latent features. The finding persists across a broad suite of planning failure endpoints, including those whose labels depend on geometry unavailable to the non-latent baseline.

## [MemProbe Diagnoses Agent Memory Tradeoffs via Cognitive Paradigms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30558) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.30558) introduces MemProbe, a cognitive-science-inspired framework that diagnoses stability-plasticity tradeoffs in agent memory systems using four experimental paradigms: interference, misinformation, consolidation strength, and reconsolidation window. The authors instantiate these paradigms in a 56-episode diagnostic suite and evaluate six incremental memory systems under a unified protocol, finding that systems with similar aggregate scores exhibit distinct behavioral profiles. Code is available at https://github.com/jq-ding/MemProbe. Current agent memory evaluations typically collapse behavior into final-answer accuracy, which hides how systems actually update, preserve, attribute, and temporally organize information. MemProbe's diagnostic lens could shift memory benchmarking toward interpretable behavioral profiles, helping researchers and practitioners choose memory architectures that match their reliability and adaptability needs. MemProbe decomposes correctness into behavioral profiles across a 56-episode suite and evaluates six incremental memory systems under one protocol, revealing that aggregate scores can mask divergent memory maintenance behavior. As an arXiv preprint without peer review or community discussion yet, its paradigms and metrics still need external validation and broader adoption.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: The stability-plasticity tradeoff is a long-standing challenge in adaptive systems: a model must retain prior knowledge (stability) while incorporating new information (plasticity), and this dilemma appears in neural networks and any distributed system that learns from local signals. Cognitive memory research holds that memory is reconstructive and shaped by interference, source reliability, reinforcement, and reactivation, with the reconsolidation window describing a brief period after recall during which a memory can be updated. MemProbe imports these ideas into AI agent memory evaluation, where long-term user preferences, task states, and evolving facts must be maintained over time.

## [Action Forcing: Training Controllable World Models on Unlabeled Video via PCA Egomotion Bases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30595) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This paper proposes a method that recovers a data-derived egomotion basis from ordinary unlabeled video using pixel displacement tracking and principal components analysis (PCA), yielding signed, scalable, and composable throttle-yaw control signals without any manual action annotations. An online latent critic then distills a frozen decoder-tracker-PCA teacher into a high-capacity video DiT, preventing the model from exploiting pixel-level supervision while enabling grounded control. Controllable world models typically require synchronized action annotations that are expensive or impossible to obtain at scale, so this work could unlock the vast supply of ordinary video for training grounded, controllable generative models. It also critiques standard video generation metrics and introduces reference-free evaluation dimensions such as controllability, plausibility, conjuring, and geometric integrity, which may shift how the field measures world-model quality. The recovered action space is limited to motion axes actually represented in the training data, and the method relies on recurring coherent structure induced by egomotion rather than learned latent actions. Despite backwards actions making up less than 1% of training data, the model learns to reverse, scale responses linearly, and compose throttle with steering, while most baselines follow familiar action directions but struggle to reverse or remain stationary.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: World models are generative models that learn to simulate environments and respond to control inputs, often used for planning or content generation. Egomotion refers to the motion of the camera or agent itself relative to the scene, which induces coherent pixel displacement patterns across video frames. Principal components analysis (PCA) is a classical dimensionality-reduction technique that finds dominant axes of variation in data; here it extracts the leading motion axes as control dimensions. Video DiT refers to diffusion transformer architectures adapted for video generation, which are high-capacity and prone to exploiting low-level supervision if not constrained.

## [KNOWS Benchmark Tests Web Agents on Knowledge Synthesis and Artifact Creation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30604) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.30604) introduces KNOWS, a benchmark of open-ended, complex browser-based tasks that require computer-use agents to retrieve information, synthesize it into artifacts like documents, presentations, and spreadsheets, and navigate program interfaces to produce a coherent final product. Each task is paired with an evaluator combining deterministic checks and LLM judgments, and frontier agents fully succeed on fewer than 3% of these long-horizon tasks. Existing computer-use agent benchmarks focus on isolated actions or question answering rather than end-to-end assistant workflows, so KNOWS exposes a major gap in how agents are evaluated. The finding that even the best agents fully succeed on fewer than 3% of tasks highlights that current agents are far from reliable as end-to-end assistants, pointing research toward tool use, visual understanding, and long-horizon reasoning. The benchmark uses a task design rubric and a validation protocol to ensure task quality, and each task's evaluator mixes deterministic checks with LLM judgments to balance richness, reliability, and automation. A notable failure mode is that errors on visual steps render final artifacts unusable even when agents complete more than 50% of other evaluation steps.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Computer-use agents are AI systems that operate software through graphical interfaces, clicking, typing, and navigating applications much like a human would. Benchmarks such as OSWorld and WebArena measure these agents on open-ended computer tasks, but they typically emphasize single actions or short interactions rather than multi-step workflows that end in a finished artifact. KNOWS targets the assistant-style workflow: searching across sources, synthesizing findings, and producing documents, presentations, or spreadsheets.

## [Framework Detects and Explains Bias in AI-Generated Python Code](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30642) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.30642) introduces a taxonomy-driven framework to identify, categorize, and explain bias in AI-generated Python code, building a ground-truth dataset by extending an existing biased-code dataset and manually annotating snippets with bias categories and human justifications. Evaluating LLMs via in-context learning, Gemini reached 80.14% classification accuracy (84.0% precision, 95.7% recall) and Qwen3-coder 82.45% accuracy (68.64% precision, 80.22% recall), with justification similarity around 80% and code identification similarity around 86-88% versus human reasoning. As LLMs become embedded in software development workflows, unintentional biases in generated code could propagate into production systems, so a systematic way to detect and explain them is important for developers, tool builders, and auditors. The results suggest LLMs can serve as automated bias-detection and justification assistants, which could support safer and fairer AI-assisted software engineering. The study uses in-context learning (ICL) rather than fine-tuning, and measures explanation quality with structured justification and code identification similarity metrics against human-authored reasoning. Notably, the open-source Qwen3-coder had higher accuracy but much lower precision than Gemini, indicating that raw accuracy alone can mask differences in false-positive behavior.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Large Language Models are increasingly used to generate code, and because they learn from large corpora of human-written code, they can reproduce social or logical biases present in that data. Prior work has documented such biases, but there has been limited systematic effort to define categories of bias in generated code and to test whether LLMs themselves can reliably detect and explain them. This paper addresses that gap with a taxonomy, a human-annotated ground-truth dataset, and an evaluation of both proprietary and open-source models.
