## [Werracle: Zero-Storage AI Oracle for EVM Smart Contracts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30719) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Werracle introduces a zero-storage on-chain AI decision oracle that fits inside a single 32-byte EVM storage slot (bytes32) and evaluates a 16-point Pareto micro-grid in only 21,438 gas with sub-millisecond latency. It derives non-linear decision hyperplanes from a 24-byte coordinate triplet using Mandelbrot escape dynamics and fixed-point Q16.16 arithmetic, and is demonstrated via a Uniswap v4 dynamic swap fee governor that adjusts LP fees between 0.05% and 0.50%. This approach addresses the latency and gas barriers that prevent on-chain AI from reacting to atomic DeFi exploits such as flash-loan attacks and sandwich MEV, which occur within a single block. By enabling sub-cent, sub-millisecond inference, it could make real-time circuit breakers and dynamic fee mechanisms practical for DeFi protocols on Layer-2 rollups. The oracle is implemented in pure Solidity bytecode (WerrMath.sol) and is formally verified against a 1,000-test cryptographically sealed deterministic verification suite with a 100.0% pass rate, with telemetry permanently disabled. It operates live on a dedicated EVM devnet sandbox (Chain ID 4242), though real-world deployment on mainnet or production L2s is not yet demonstrated.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Storing neural network weight matrices directly in EVM storage is prohibitively expensive, costing millions of gas, which makes direct on-chain AI inference impractical. Zero-Knowledge Machine Learning (ZK-ML) offloads computation to off-chain provers but introduces 10–300 seconds of SNARK proving latency and 250,000–500,000 gas per proof verification, too slow to react to atomic DeFi exploits. Flash loans allow attackers to borrow large sums without collateral within a single transaction, enabling price manipulation and other attacks that execute atomically inside one block.

## [TrafficImag: First Benchmark for Counterfactual Roadside Traffic Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30722) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced TrafficImag, the first benchmark for counterfactual roadside traffic video generation, combining a large-scale dataset of 9,022 annotated images, 7,043 deduplicated video clips, and 31,145 actor-centered history-future samples with an executable protocol for behavior reasoning, intervention-aware image editing, and conditional video generation. Each intervention is encoded as an actor-level program specifying the target actor, intended behavior, legal route, interaction order, and temporal constraints, and evaluation covers four validity dimensions: initial-state correctness, route and behavior validity, interaction consistency, and non-target preservation. This benchmark fills a gap in existing roadside traffic datasets, which support perception, forecasting, and visual question answering but not counterfactual video generation, and it provides a reproducible evaluation interface across heterogeneous foundation models. It could spur further research in autonomous driving, video generation, and AI benchmarking by enabling diagnosis of counterfactual traffic video generation beyond perceptual video quality. Across state-of-the-art foundation models, the strongest reasoner reaches 80.4% macro F1, and the complete condition interface raises end-to-end success from 23.3% to 55.0% for the best generator. Oracle studies further show that conditional video execution is the primary remaining bottleneck, and an end-to-end counterfactual is considered successful only when all four validity dimensions are satisfied.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Counterfactual video generation creates modified versions of real videos in which a chosen actor's behavior is changed while the rest of the scene remains consistent, which is useful for testing how models reason about cause and effect. Roadside traffic datasets typically provide fixed recordings for perception, forecasting, and visual question answering, but they lack a way to evaluate such counterfactual generation. TrafficImag addresses this by defining interventions as actor-level programs and evaluating whether generated futures respect road topology, unrelated traffic, and temporal constraints.

## [Anatomy-Aware Design Optimization for Surgical Continuum Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30745) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces the Reachable Volumetric Dexterous Solid Angle (RVDSA) metric, which measures how well a robot's end effector can reach points in a goal volume from different directions via collision-free paths. Using this metric with an asymptotically optimal simulated annealing optimizer, the authors optimized a bimanual dexterous sheaths robot for colon polyp procedures and achieved 78% higher RVDSA on average than optimizing for 3D voxel coverage alone. This work addresses a key challenge in surgical robotics: designing continuum robots that are dexterous enough for specific anatomical environments. The anatomy-aware optimization could lead to more effective robot designs for minimally invasive procedures, potentially improving outcomes for colon polyp removal and other delicate interventions. The RVDSA metric evaluates dexterity by considering both reachability and the diversity of approach directions from a start configuration, and the authors developed a computationally efficient motion planner to compute it. The optimization uses simulated annealing, which is asymptotically optimal, and the method was validated on a bimanual robot for colon anatomies.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Continuum robots are flexible, snake-like robots often used in minimally invasive surgery because they can navigate narrow, curved anatomical spaces. Their performance depends heavily on geometric design parameters, such as link lengths and joint placements, which must be chosen to ensure dexterity within a specific patient anatomy. Traditional design optimization often focuses on simple coverage metrics like 3D voxel coverage, which ignore the ability to approach targets from multiple directions. This paper proposes a new metric and optimization framework to better capture dexterity in real anatomical environments.

## [SustainAI: A Water-Aware, Closed-Loop Framework for Accountable AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30747) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced SustainAI, a water-aware, closed-loop framework that integrates real-time water metering, a hallucination-aware penalty model, and a water-aware routing algorithm to embed environmental accountability into AI deployment. Evaluated using Small Language Models extracting health misinformation, the system revealed an 11-fold variation in water footprint across geographically distributed data centers, ranging from 0.0477 mL to 0.5360 mL per inference. This work addresses a largely invisible aspect of AI's environmental footprint—freshwater consumption—which is often overshadowed by energy and carbon concerns. By showing that routing decisions can significantly reduce water use and that substantial resources are wasted on inaccurate outputs, it offers a practical path toward more sustainable and ethically grounded AI infrastructure. Across 1,335 inference runs, the system consumed approximately 399 mL of water but produced only 240 correct outputs, highlighting inefficiency in resource use. The framework also incorporates a 'Care by Design' lens that frames AI sustainability around relational ethics, regional equity, and ecological stewardship, extending beyond purely technical optimization.

The authors also propose a style-diversification method to scale up long-tail data, and report that a model trained on this dataset achieves a 75% success rate in real-world flying experiments. The dataset is evaluated against existing UAV navigation datasets across multiple metrics, and the model trained on NavGen data generally improves with scale, outperforming those trained on prior datasets. Real-world transferability is validated by deploying the trained model in a world-action-model paradigm for actual flying experiments across different tasks and environments.

## [LIRSeg Replaces Chain-of-Thought with Latent Tokens for Reasoning Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30783) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces LIRSeg, a method that fully replaces explicit Chain-of-Thought (CoT) with a compact set of learnable latent tokens for reasoning segmentation in multimodal large language models (MLLMs). Trained in two stages—spatial alignment followed by GRPO optimization—LIRSeg achieves absolute gIoU improvements of 4.9% on ReasonSeg, 7.1% on MUSE, and 4.7% on MMR over the VisionReasoner baseline, while reducing reasoning tokens by approximately 16x. This work addresses a key inefficiency in multimodal reasoning: explicit verbal CoT introduces attention interference by flooding the model with redundant text tokens that disrupt visual perception. By showing that compact latent tokens can outperform verbose reasoning while cutting token usage by 16x, LIRSeg could influence how future MLLMs balance reasoning depth with computational efficiency in applications like human-computer interaction and embodied agents. To keep the compact latent tokens informative, the authors introduce three mechanisms: extreme-advantage sampling for selecting informative training signals, decoupled exploration-stability updates for learning complementary representations, and latent diversity amplification to prevent representational collapse. The code is available in supplementary materials, and the method is evaluated on ReasonSeg, MUSE, and MMR benchmarks.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Reasoning segmentation requires a model to interpret implicit textual queries and produce fine-grained pixel-level masks, which is important for applications such as human-computer interaction and embodied agents. Existing approaches typically have a multimodal large language model generate an explicit Chain-of-Thought—a step-by-step verbal reasoning trace—before localizing the target. However, this explicit verbal reasoning introduces attention interference: redundant textual tokens disrupt attention during perception-token generation and increase the effective distance between visual tokens. GRPO (Group Relative Policy Optimization) is a reinforcement learning algorithm that computes policy gradients using group-normalized advantage estimation without a value critic, standardizing rewards within candidate groups to stabilize learning.

## [iDriveVLA Tackles Generation-Evaluation Asymmetry in Multi-Modal Driving Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30818) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces iDriveVLA, a multi-modal planning framework that addresses a pronounced 'generation-evaluation asymmetry' in autonomous driving, where planners with strong oracle performance still fail to reliably select the best candidate trajectory. iDriveVLA combines a Safety-aware Scorer for quality and risk estimation with a VLM-guided Modulator for scene-adaptive criterion weighting, and achieves a new state-of-the-art 94.95 PDMS on the public NAVSIM v1 leaderboard, surpassing the human-expert reference. This work shifts attention from merely generating diverse trajectories to reliably evaluating and selecting among them, a bottleneck that has limited the real-world benefit of multi-modal planners in ambiguous and long-tail driving scenarios. If validated, the approach could improve safety and decision reliability for autonomous driving and robotics systems that rely on vision-language models for planning. iDriveVLA uses a unified trajectory evaluator with an oracle-aligned progressive training strategy consisting of candidate imitation pretraining, candidate space refinement, and semantic ranking alignment. Its reported 94.95 PDMS on NAVSIM v1 is a preprint result and has not yet undergone peer review.

**Background**: Multi-modal planning in autonomous driving generates multiple plausible future trajectories to handle ambiguous and long-tail scenarios, rather than committing to a single predicted path. NAVSIM is a public benchmark for evaluating such planners, and PDMS (Predictive Driver Model Score) is its composite metric for driving performance.

## [MOPD-Router Enables Label-Free Token-Level Teacher Routing for Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30837) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced MOPD-Router, a framework for multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD) that routes supervision from the full teacher pool at each token without requiring prompt-level domain labels or training a separate routing model. Its plug-in interface supports different routing metrics, and the proposed ExpertAlign metric scores each teacher by whether its correction expresses the specialization acquired during post-training; on unlabeled data it improves the overall score by 5.88 points (+12.3%) over Mean aggregation, and on domain-labeled data it beats standard MOPD by 3.95 points (+7.8%) without using the available labels. This work removes a key bottleneck in multi-teacher distillation: the reliance on prompt-level domain labels, which restricts training on unlabeled mixtures and wastes complementary signals from non-matched teachers. By enabling token-level routing, it could make LLM post-training more flexible and data-efficient, benefiting researchers and engineers who combine multiple specialized models into a single student. ExpertAlign is compared against two reference metrics based on teacher confidence (Entropy) and teacher-student discrepancy (Novelty), and it achieves the strongest overall performance in all four settings spanning unlabeled and domain-labeled mixtures under strong-to-weak and same-size distillation scenarios. The framework is a plug-in interface, and code is available at https://github.com/TURLEing/MOPD-Router.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Knowledge distillation trains a smaller student model to mimic a larger teacher, and on-policy distillation does this on the student's own generated rollouts. Multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD) extends this by combining several specialized teachers, such as models fine-tuned for math or instruction following, into one student. Existing MOPD practice typically hard-routes each prompt to a single domain-matched teacher for the whole rollout, which requires domain labels and ignores other teachers' complementary corrections.

## [UltraG-Bench: A New Benchmark for Pixel-level Grounding in Ultrasound VLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30928) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced UltraG-Bench, a large-scale multi-task benchmark for evaluating pixel-level evidence grounding in ultrasound images, built by annotating 40 public ultrasound segmentation datasets across 13 anatomical categories. It comprises three progressive tasks—instruction-guided segmentation (331,125 annotations), evidence-grounded VQA (666,779 annotations), and evidence-grounded report generation (138,832 annotations)—and evaluation of 14 state-of-the-art models reveals a substantial gap between semantic understanding and fine-grained pixel-level localization. The team also proposed UltraG-Agent, which combines a VLM's semantic reasoning with the ultrasound-specific segmentation capability of UltraSAM3, substantially improving both semantic prediction and pixel-level grounding. This benchmark addresses a critical blind spot in medical AI evaluation: current vision-language models can describe ultrasound findings but cannot reliably point to the exact pixels supporting their claims, which is essential for clinical trust and regulatory acceptance. By providing over 1.1 million annotations and a standardized multi-task evaluation framework, UltraG-Bench could drive research toward models that produce verifiable, evidence-grounded outputs in medical imaging. The benchmark spans 13 anatomical categories and three tasks of increasing difficulty, with the evidence-grounded VQA task containing the largest annotation count at 666,779. The proposed UltraG-Agent pairs a general VLM with UltraSAM3, an ultrasound-specific segmentation foundation model, suggesting that specialized segmentation modules are needed to compensate for VLMs' weak pixel-level grounding. All data and code are publicly available on GitHub.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Ultrasound is one of the most widely used medical imaging modalities, and large vision-language models (VLMs) have recently shown growing ability to understand ultrasound images. Pixel-level evidence grounding refers to a model's ability to localize the exact image regions that support its textual or semantic predictions, a capability known as visual grounding. While VLMs excel at semantic tasks like visual question answering, prior work has shown that high text accuracy does not guarantee correct pixel-level grounding, and this gap has been largely unexplored in ultrasound. UltraSAM3 builds on the UltraSam line of ultrasound foundation models, which were trained on large collections of open-access ultrasound segmentation datasets.

## [EoupCT Estimates and Orthogonalizes Unknown Pre-training Gradients for Continual LLM Fine-tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30935) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose EoupCT, a framework that estimates unknown pre-training gradients by dynamically generating pseudo data most susceptible to forgetting, using a learnable soft prompt with Gumbel-Softmax relaxation. It formulates a multi-objective optimization problem and introduces a first-order efficient Pareto optimizer that jointly optimizes LLM parameters and the soft prompt, enforcing orthogonality between new task updates and the estimated pre-training gradients. Continual fine-tuning of LLMs inevitably causes catastrophic forgetting, degrading both previous task performance and the model's general-purpose knowledge. Existing orthogonal gradient projection methods fail to preserve pre-training knowledge because the original pre-training data and gradients are unknown, so EoupCT addresses a critical gap that affects anyone deploying LLMs in dynamic real-world environments. The method relies on a learnable soft prompt with Gumbel-Softmax relaxation to generate pseudo data, and uses a first-order efficient Pareto optimizer to jointly update LLM parameters and the prompt while enforcing orthogonality. Extensive experiments across multiple LLMs reportedly show it preserves both task-specific proficiency and inherent general-purpose knowledge, though the work is an arXiv preprint without peer review or community validation yet.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Catastrophic forgetting occurs when an LLM updates its weights to learn new information, causing interference with previously learned knowledge because the model stores knowledge in a single interconnected system. Continual learning aims to let models acquire new tasks sequentially without losing old capabilities, and gradient orthogonalization is a common technique that projects new task updates away from directions important for old tasks. However, for off-the-shelf pre-trained LLMs, the original pre-training data and gradients are unknown, making direct orthogonalization against pre-training knowledge impossible.

## [OneWorld Enforces Consistent Physics Across Actions in World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30946) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.30946) introduces OneWorld, a shared-mechanism counterfactual generation framework that jointly models multiple action-conditioned futures under a common latent physical mechanism. A physical mechanism interpreter infers a distribution over latent mechanisms for each action-outcome branch, aggregates them into shared-world evidence, and uses this evidence to constrain flow training and guide sampling, improving cross-intervention physical consistency while keeping single-rollout prediction quality competitive. This addresses a core reliability problem in model-based reinforcement learning and planning: independently generated futures from the same scene can each look plausible yet imply incompatible physical properties such as friction or mass, leading to contradictory predictions across interventions. By enforcing a shared physical explanation, OneWorld could make world models more trustworthy for decision-making, robot planning, and counterfactual reasoning. The method aggregates per-branch latent mechanism distributions into shared-world evidence that accounts for uncertainty in less informative branches, then uses this evidence to constrain flow training and sampling-time guidance while preserving action-induced differences. The authors also introduce a multi-intervention evaluation protocol in controlled environments following ACWM-Phys interaction settings, assessing whether generated futures can be jointly explained by the same physical parameters alongside standard single-rollout prediction quality.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Action-conditioned video world models are generative systems that predict future video frames conditioned on past actions, often by fine-tuning large pretrained video models with lightweight action-embedding networks. They act as learned simulators for agents, enabling planning and reinforcement learning without a physical environment. A key challenge is that different action-conditioned rollouts from the same initial scene may be physically inconsistent, undermining the model's coherent understanding of the underlying world.

## [MVVBench: A New Benchmark for 4D Multi-View Video Reasoning in VLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30952) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduce MVVBench, a benchmark built from real-world multi-camera datasets that evaluates multi-view video reasoning in vision-language models. Each question is deliberately designed to be unanswerable from any single camera view, and most are also unanswerable from any single moment, so they can only be solved by jointly reasoning across views and time. This benchmark fills a gap in evaluating 4D reasoning, since most existing video benchmarks can be solved from a single view or a single frame. It provides a rigorous testbed for embodied perception and multi-camera understanding, which matters for robotics, autonomous driving, and surveillance applications. MVVBench probes six capabilities: implicit and explicit attribute identification, implicit and explicit relative distance, relative camera pose, and compositional counting, all with human-authored QA and rigorous verification. The paper also analyzes failure modes such as temporal mis-localization, cross-view identity breaks, and brittle multi-hop reasoning, and shows that inference-time chain-of-thought scaffolds and structured cross-view evidence aggregation yield substantial gains without retraining.

Multi-view video understanding requires integrating spatial and temporal evidence across multiple, often non-overlapping camera streams, tracking entities as they move between viewpoints and reasoning about latent 4D continuity rather than any single visible frame. MVVBench is designed to test exactly this capability.

## [Time Conditioning May Be Unnecessary in Uniform Discrete Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30977) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.30977) shows that explicit time conditioning in uniform discrete diffusion models (UDMs) is often unnecessary for language tasks. The authors prove that while the population-optimal predictor depends on time, this dependence becomes negligible in finite-data settings, and time-agnostic predictors match or outperform time-conditioned models across datasets and training objectives. This finding challenges a long-standing assumption in diffusion modeling that explicit time conditioning is required to guide the denoising process. If confirmed, it could simplify UDM architectures, reduce training complexity, and improve performance for language generation, affecting researchers and practitioners working on discrete diffusion and text generation. The theoretical analysis shows that the empirical-optimal predictor is nearly time-insensitive over most of the diffusion trajectory when a corrupted training sequence remains much closer to its original clean sequence than to competing sequences, though the guarantee weakens toward the high-noise endpoint. Empirically, trained language UDMs exhibit limited time sensitivity, and time-agnostic predictors remain competitive or better across datasets and objectives.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Uniform discrete diffusion models (UDMs) are a class of generative models that operate on discrete states, such as tokens in language, by gradually corrupting data and learning to reverse the process. Unlike continuous diffusion models used for images, UDMs work with uniform transition probabilities over discrete states. Time conditioning, where the model is told the current noise level, is standard practice in diffusion models to guide denoising, but this paper questions whether it is needed for language UDMs.

## [FARE Detects Bait-and-Switch Swaps in Deployed AI Image Generators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30982) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose FARE (Forensic Acceptance Region Estimation), a method that enrolls a certified image generator by training on its sampled images and then judges, from a single generated image alone, whether the deployed service still matches that certified generator. FARE amplifies generator-specific forensic artifacts via hard sample mining to tighten the acceptance region, and it consistently outperforms existing baselines at strict operating points across generator swaps, including similar model versions and variants. Because modern image generators are exposed as opaque APIs where customers cannot inspect weights or architecture, a provider could pass governance certification with one model and silently deploy a cheaper, lower-quality one. FARE offers a deployment-time integrity auditing tool that supports AI governance, model auditing, and security in high-stakes domains where public trust or safety is at risk. FARE requires only images from a single certified generator for enrollment and calibration, with no access to the deployed model's weights or architecture, and it remains effective under the exact-model and decision-only attacks evaluated in the paper. The work is an arXiv preprint (arXiv:2609.30982) without community discussion or engagement metrics, so its real-world impact will depend on follow-up validation and adoption.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: AI image generators are increasingly offered as opaque APIs, meaning users can query the service but cannot see the underlying model weights or architecture. Forensic methods for synthetic-image detection typically rely on generator-specific artifacts, subtle statistical traces left by a particular model's generation process. FARE builds on this idea by learning an "acceptance region" of such artifacts for a certified generator, so that images falling outside that region signal a possible model swap.

## [A Signature-Based Linear Representation Hypothesis for VLA Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30996) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.30996) develops a theoretical, signature-based formulation of the linear representation hypothesis (LRH) specifically for vision-language-action (VLA) models, unifying representations and policies. It proves that representations exist from which a physical quantity of interest's future evolution under a candidate action trajectory can be recovered via linear probing, and introduces a signature generalized linear model for stochastic action chunks that enables linear steering. This extends the LRH — a standard interpretability lens for large language models — into embodied AI, where physical quantities evolve jointly with system dynamics rather than being static semantic attributes. It could provide a principled foundation for measuring and steering VLA internals, benefiting interpretability and robot learning research. The formulation is signature-based and treats VLA models as controlled dynamical systems, with the policy side modeled as a signature generalized linear model for stochastic action chunks. The authors construct an explicit oracle representation in a planar control-affine navigation experiment and verify the predicted linear probing and steering mechanisms, though results remain theoretical and simulation-based.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

Vision-language-action (VLA) models are multimodal foundation models that take images and text instructions and directly output low-level robot actions, pioneered by Google DeepMind's RT-2 in 2023. Unlike static semantic attributes such as gender or language in LLMs, a physical quantity of interest in a VLA evolves with the system dynamics, since representations influence actions that alter physical state and thus the next representation.

## [Minimax Limits and Black-Box Attacks in Passive Image Provenance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30997) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.30997) derives the exact minimax limit for any image-only provenance verifier: the largest robust target-acceptance gap equals the minimum total-variation distance between the target distribution and the set of attacked source distributions. It also shows that if a deployed verifier can be emulated on the attack region to error ε, a surrogate black-box attack reaches within 2ε of the white-box optimum, and demonstrates that public CLIP-based verifiers fail under targeted pixel attacks. This work provides a theoretical ceiling for passive image provenance, showing that no verifier architecture can exceed a statistical limit determined by the source, target, and edit class. It also explains why deployed verifiers can fail well before that limit, which has direct implications for the reliability of AI-generated content detection and for how provenance systems should be evaluated. The minimax identity is verified in a finite-state experiment where both sides are computable, and score-revealing logistic and softmax heads over public features are shown to be identifiable, with stable recovery bounds under approximate score access. On same-prompt real/diffusion benchmarks, public CLIP verifiers fail under targeted pixel attacks, while a ResNet-18 victim shows partial fake-to-real transfer; binary feedback with abstention reduces measured attack success, but positive empirical gap upper bounds do not establish robustness.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Passive image provenance asks whether pixels alone can reveal an image's origin—human, aggregate AI class, or a specific generator—without embedded watermarks or metadata. Once an attacker can edit the image before verification, provenance becomes a source-target verification problem under adversarial distribution shift. Minimax analysis from statistical learning theory characterizes the best possible worst-case performance, while total variation distance measures the distinguishability of probability distributions. Black-box adversarial attacks assume the attacker sees only model outputs, not weights or gradients.

## [G²PTQ: Generalized Gradient Compensation for Better LLM Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31009) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced G²PTQ, a unified post-training quantization (PTQ) framework that combines first- and second-order information under a globally supervised, block-wise optimization objective. It refreshes gradient and Hessian estimates before quantizing each Transformer block and adds a trust-region scaling mechanism to prevent exploding weight updates, with code released at github.com/G2PTQ/G2PTQ. PTQ is a practical way to shrink LLM memory and compute without retraining, and GPTQ-style methods are the de facto standard, so improving their accuracy directly benefits anyone deploying large models on limited hardware. G²PTQ reportedly aligns better with full-precision models and outperforms state-of-the-art baselines across model families and bit-widths. The framework addresses two complementary flaws in prior work: local layer-wise methods lack global supervision, while global methods freeze Hessian estimates at the start and ignore first-order gradients, causing stale guidance. G²PTQ derives efficient implementations for block-wise Hessian approximation and exact first-order gradient compensation, using trust-region scaling to bound the gradient step.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Post-training quantization (PTQ) compresses a model after training by representing weights in low-precision formats such as 4-bit, reducing memory and speeding up inference without retraining. GPTQ is a widely used PTQ method that quantizes weights layer by layer, using the inverse Hessian matrix (which captures the local curvature of the loss) to decide how sensitive each weight is and to compensate errors by updating remaining unquantized weights. G²PTQ builds on this Optimal Brain Quantization idea but adds global supervision and refreshed first- and second-order estimates.

## [DynBranch Cuts Agentic LLM Latency by 32% via Speculative Subgraph Reuse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31047) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.31047) introduces DynBranch, a system that makes an unresolved branch in agentic LLM workflows addressable before it resolves, allowing candidate subgraphs to execute speculatively and completed results to be reused across later requests. Tested on four agentic workloads with Qwen3-32B on 4x H200 GPUs, it reduces mean latency by up to 32% over each workload's strongest prior system and by 46-66% against a no-reuse floor, with benefits also observed on a commodity Qwen3-8B/RTX 4090 setup. Agentic LLM workflows increasingly dominate production serving, but their runtime branch decisions create serialization stalls that caching alone cannot hide. DynBranch attacks this bottleneck at the model-API boundary without modifying agent harnesses or execution engines, making it a drop-in optimization that could broadly improve latency and throughput for multi-step agent deployments. DynBranch uses a stable coordinate to name candidate work before branch resolution, then authorizes execution via an exact input match with fresh dependencies; a two-level controller admits speculative work only when its expected benefit exceeds the current load price. The approach preserves workflow results and generalizes across backbone model families, though it is still a preprint without peer review or community discussion.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Agentic LLM workflows are multi-step pipelines in which a model decides at runtime which path to take, for example choosing a tool or sub-task based on intermediate output. This runtime decision creates a branch-resolution barrier: downstream computation cannot start until the branch is resolved, and traditional caching fails because the cache key is unknown until then. Speculative execution, familiar from CPU design, runs likely work ahead of time to hide such latency, and DynBranch adapts this idea to LLM serving by treating unresolved branches as reusable serving state.

## [Hybrid Quantum Diffusion Model Scales to Real Medical Images](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31070) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.31070) introduces a scalable hybrid Quantum Diffusion Model that uses a Discrete-Time Quantum Walk executed on a real quantum device for the forward diffusion process, paired with a classical denoising model for the backward step. Unlike prior quantum machine learning attempts limited by small quantum hardware, this method processes real-world large grayscale and RGB images as well as moderate-size 3D medical volumes, and benchmarks competitively against a classical discrete-state diffusion counterpart on three standard image-generation metrics. This work addresses the long-standing scalability bottleneck of quantum machine learning for image analysis, showing that hybrid quantum-classical diffusion can handle practical medical imaging data rather than only toy examples. It could accelerate interest in quantum-assisted generative models for healthcare, where large 3D volumes such as CT and MRI scans are routine. The forward process is a Discrete-Time Quantum Walk run on real quantum hardware, while the backward denoising step is entirely classical, making the approach a hybrid rather than fully quantum pipeline. Evaluation covers grayscale, RGB, and moderate-size 3D volumes, with comparisons to a classical discrete-state diffusion model using three state-of-the-art image-generation metrics; the paper does not claim quantum advantage, only competitive and promising results.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Diffusion models are generative models that gradually add noise to data (forward process) and then learn to reverse that noise (backward process) to generate new samples. Quantum machine learning aims to exploit quantum phenomena such as superposition, entanglement, and interference to improve learning tasks, but existing quantum devices are small and noisy, which has severely limited image-analysis applications. A Discrete-Time Quantum Walk is a quantum analogue of a classical random walk, and hybrid quantum-classical approaches embed quantum subroutines inside classical optimization loops to work within current hardware constraints.

DepthEvidence is a 4B multimodal language model that uses its own dense metric depth predictions as object-grounded evidence for language generation, paired with a new Depth-VQA benchmark. The model uses a camera-conditioned decoder with multi-scale visual features and high-resolution RGB refinement to predict full-resolution metric depth, then converts depths and decoder features into object-aligned continuous geometry tokens anchored to object identifiers. Geometric supervision keeps metric information recoverable before and after language-context interaction, while instruction tuning supports object measurement and compositional reasoning.

**Background**: Metric depth prediction estimates the actual distance from the camera to each pixel, unlike relative depth which only preserves ordering. DepthEvidence bridges this gap by feeding its own depth predictions into the language model as structured evidence, and Depth-VQA evaluates object-depth queries, relative comparisons, and decisions combining spatial and numerical constraints.

## [Fisher Information Geometry Unifies Bayesian Optimization Trust-Region Methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31107) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.31107) studies Bayesian optimization through information geometry, pulling back the Fisher information metric through the surrogate posterior map to obtain a local sensitivity tensor on the input space. This yields an upper bound on the gradient of reparameterizable acquisition functions and motivates FITR, a trust-region BO method that replaces lengthscale-based scaling with local pullback-Fisher weights. The work offers a common theoretical explanation for heuristics like RAASP and dimension-scaled lengthscales, and explains vanishing gradients in high-dimensional BO. FITR generalizes beyond Gaussian process kernels with explicit lengthscales, potentially broadening the applicability of trust-region BO to non-isotropic surrogates. FITR is not restricted to GP kernels with explicit lengthscales and easily generalizes to non-isotropic surrogates, though gains in that setting are more task-dependent. On GP benchmarks with a squared-exponential kernel, experiments show competitive performance using FITR.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Bayesian optimization (BO) is a sample-efficient method for optimizing expensive black-box functions, typically using a Gaussian process surrogate and an acquisition function to decide where to sample next. Trust-region methods constrain each optimization step to a local region to stabilize updates, and are widely used in high-dimensional BO. Information geometry studies probability distributions as points on a Riemannian manifold, where the Fisher information metric measures local distances between distributions; pulling this metric back to the input space gives a sensitivity measure for the surrogate model.

## [Discrete Neural Insertion Sort Fixes Shortcut Learning in NAR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31114) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper analyzes shortcut learning in the CLRS30 baseline for neural algorithmic reasoning (NAR) on insertion sort, showing that the hint objective is weakly optimized and that intermediate representations can be decoded into sorted sequences before the reference execution terminates. To address this, the authors introduce Discrete Neural Insertion Sort, which represents the sequence as a chain, separates scalar exchanges from control-state transitions, and projects node representations back to discrete states after every processor step. This work matters because it shows that correct final outputs and intermediate supervision do not guarantee a model actually follows the intended algorithm, a core concern for neural algorithmic reasoning. The proposed discrete approach achieves 100% sorted-sequence accuracy on lengths 64 and 128 when trained only on length 16, demonstrating strong length generalization that could inform more faithful and generalizable neural algorithm learners. An ablation reveals that discretization and graph structure alone are insufficient: without additional supervision of the global inner-loop state, the model fails even at the training length. This highlights that discrete execution can support strong length generalization but requires problem-specific inductive bias to learn a faithful algorithmic execution.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Neural algorithmic reasoning (NAR) aims to train neural networks to execute known algorithms and generalize beyond the input sizes seen during training. The CLRS Algorithmic Reasoning Benchmark is a standardized suite covering classical algorithms from Introduction to Algorithms, providing stepwise execution traces for evaluation. Shortcut learning refers to neural networks exploiting simpler patterns that minimize loss rather than learning the intended reasoning process, a known limitation in deep learning.

## [JevAdvBench: First Adversarial Benchmark for RLCD Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31142) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces JevAdvBench, the first adversarial benchmark for reinforcement learning for calibrated decisions (RLCD) models, featuring 812 typed questions across 66 scenarios and a black-box attack suite of 9,744 single-edit variants. It also proposes a novel scoring method that compares each attacked decision against the model's own clean decision rather than against labels, and tests on jev-1.13.0 show that an unverified opinion appended to the state flips 12.1% of decisions. This work fills a critical gap in robustness measurement for RLCD models, which are increasingly used in automated decision pipelines where outputs are consumed directly by software without human review. The findings suggest that applications built on RLCD models should treat the state as untrusted, argued input, with significant implications for AI safety and adversarial machine learning research. On jev-1.13.0, rewording stays within 1.2 percentage points of the re-run baseline and fields outside the schema never reach the model, but one unverified opinion appended to the state flips 12.1% of decisions—statistically tied with the strongest injected command at 10.1%—and pushes 38% of confident answers below the 0.8 confidence threshold that routes them to human review. The benchmark uses billed input tokens to confirm that each edit reached the model, addressing the challenge that identical requests can return different answers and that the API preprocesses requests out of view.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Reinforcement learning for calibrated decisions (RLCD) is a training method used by TypeSafe AI's Jev model, which returns typed values such as probabilities, choices, or scores rather than generating natural-language text, so its output can be consumed directly by software. Unlike large language models, a typed model generates nothing and returns a well-formed answer even when manipulated, making robustness measurement difficult because identical requests can return different answers and most available labels come from the model itself. Jev was released in limited early access on 15 September 2026 alongside a $40 million seed round led by DCVC.

## [SPADE: A Pareto-Frontier Metric for Serendipitous Recommendations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31164) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces SPADE (Serendipitous Pareto Distance Evaluation), a metric that maps items into a two-dimensional popularity-similarity space and computes a user-specific Pareto frontier of maximally popular and historically similar items. The serendipity score is the average minimum Euclidean distance from this frontier, calculated strictly for correctly recommended test-set items, and it was validated across five datasets and five baseline algorithms. Existing beyond-accuracy metrics for serendipity often isolate either historical similarity or global popularity, making them easy for algorithms to game with irrelevant or non-personalized recommendations. SPADE addresses this gap by jointly considering similarity, popularity, and actual user relevance, which could give recommender-system researchers and practitioners a more reliable way to evaluate whether systems truly surface surprising yet useful items. The metric only scores correctly recommended test-set items, so it cannot be inflated by irrelevant or non-personalized suggestions, and the authors report that it successfully prevents such exploitation across five datasets and five baseline algorithms. The approach relies on Euclidean distance in a two-dimensional popularity-similarity space and on a user-specific Pareto frontier, meaning results depend on how popularity and similarity are defined and normalized.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Recommender systems are usually judged by accuracy metrics such as precision or recall, but researchers also study beyond-accuracy goals like diversity, novelty, and serendipity. Serendipity refers to a system's ability to surprise users with unexpected yet relevant recommendations that are not obvious from their past behavior. A Pareto frontier, borrowed from multi-objective optimization, is the set of solutions where no option can be improved on one objective without worsening another, and it is used here to represent the best possible trade-off between popularity and similarity for each user.

## [BAT-CLIP: First Trimodal Brain-Audio-Text Alignment for iEEG Speech Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31180) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose BAT-CLIP, the first CLIP-style trimodal alignment framework for intracranial EEG (iEEG) that jointly aligns neural embeddings to both pretrained audio and text anchors within a shared, frozen audio-text manifold. On the naturalistic Podcast benchmark, BAT-CLIP produces more robust representations than bimodal CLIP baselines that anchor to only audio or only text. Brain-speech decoding has been forced into a trade-off where audio anchoring preserves temporal structure but weakens linguistic separability, while text anchoring captures semantics but discards acoustic detail; jointly aligning to both anchors could resolve this tension. This matters for brain-computer interfaces and multimodal AI, since more robust neural representations could improve naturalistic speech decoding for clinical and assistive applications. BAT-CLIP aligns iEEG embeddings to audio and text anchors that live in a shared, frozen audio-text manifold, meaning the pretrained audio and text encoders are not fine-tuned during CLIP training. The paper also emphasizes that using self-supervised foundation models as the anchor encoders is important for effective CLIP training, and evaluation is limited to the naturalistic Podcast benchmark.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Intracranial EEG (iEEG) is an invasive neurophysiological technique that records brain activity directly with millisecond temporal precision and millimeter spatial specificity, often in patients undergoing epilepsy monitoring. CLIP (Contrastive Language-Image Pretraining) is a multimodal representation-learning paradigm that uses a contrastive objective to align two modalities—originally images and text—into a shared embedding space. Recent brain-speech research adapts this idea by aligning neural activity to pretrained speech or language representation spaces, but typically to only one anchor modality at a time. Self-supervised foundation models are large neural architectures trained on unlabeled data to capture general, transferable signal patterns, which makes them attractive as frozen anchor encoders.

## [Practitioner Study Redefines Data Quality for AI Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31191) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.31191) reports interviews with 16 practitioners from nine organizations, analyzed via reflexive thematic analysis, yielding six themes on how data quality is redefined in AI-driven software-intensive systems. Key shifts include traceability moving from modular debugging to attributing model behavior, circularity when models assess data quality, agent context and memory becoming data objects, authenticity concerns from synthetic and pseudo-labeled data, and lawfulness acting as a gate for foundation-model training data. This empirical study bridges a gap between separate ML research threads and real engineering practice, showing that data quality in AI systems is now an organizational and lifecycle concern rather than a purely technical input-validation problem. Its findings can inform AI engineering, data governance, and assurance practices, especially as foundation models and agents become more widespread. The study synthesizes its six themes through a conceptual framing called lifecycle assurance, focused on producing evidence that data can support a specific AI claim when its influence may be embedded in model behavior, model-based judgments, or agent actions. It also identifies five recurring conditions that help explain reduced trust in data and AI outcomes, though the paper is qualitative and based on only 16 interviews, limiting generalizability.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Data quality research has traditionally treated data as an input that is stored, processed, and validated. In AI-driven software-intensive systems, however, data also shapes model behavior, evaluation, and lawful use, creating new quality dimensions. Reflexive thematic analysis is a qualitative method for identifying and interpreting patterns of meaning in interview transcripts, while pseudo-labeling uses model-generated labels on unlabeled data, and lawfulness refers to legal bases such as GDPR for using training data.

## [Acoustic-to-Text KV Compression for Full-Duplex Speech Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31224) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.31224) proposes acoustic-to-text KV compression, which uses a transcription side channel to convert incoming speech into compact textual memory during listening-time slack, evicting older acoustic KV states once the cache exceeds a target budget. Trained with LoRA and knowledge distillation on MiniCPM-o 4.5, it cuts peak streaming KV-cache size by 64.6% on ten-minute LongSpeech sessions while improving transcription, temporal QA, and summarization. Full-duplex speech models continuously accumulate acoustic KV states, making long-running spoken interactions memory-intensive and expensive to serve. This approach shows that exploiting otherwise idle listening intervals can substantially shrink the KV cache without degrading turn-taking, pause handling, or interruption behavior, which matters for deploying real-time streaming voice assistants at scale. The side channel is trained with LoRA using cross-entropy on transcription segments, while knowledge distillation matches the original model's token-level output distributions at native prediction positions to preserve listening and speaking behavior. Full-Duplex-Bench evaluations show comparable pause-handling, turn-taking, and interruption performance, though results are reported only for the MiniCPM-o 4.5 implementation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Full-duplex speech models can listen and speak simultaneously, unlike cascaded assistants that wait for audio, transcribe it, run a language model, and then synthesize speech. In transformer-based models, the KV cache stores key-value states for all previously processed tokens, and because speech encodes into far longer sequences than text, this cache grows quickly during long conversations. LoRA is a parameter-efficient fine-tuning method that freezes pre-trained weights and injects small trainable low-rank matrices, while knowledge distillation transfers the output behavior of a larger or original model to a modified one.

The paper introduces DeformView, a wide-baseline multi-view dataset with pixel-level annotations of geometric inconsistencies, and proposes DEFECt3R, a lightweight learning-based classifier that localizes such inconsistencies at the pixel level. DEFECt3R outperforms existing multi-view consistency-scoring methods, substantially reducing false positives by learning from explicit supervision including hard negatives from geometrically consistent yet deformed views. This work addresses an underexplored area in multimedia forensics, where multi-view geometric consistency has potential as a forensic signal but lacks dedicated benchmarks and methods. By providing a dataset and baseline, it could spur further research into detecting manipulated or synthetically generated multi-view imagery. The dataset focuses on wide-baseline image pairs, which are challenging due to large disparities and occlusions, and the paper shows that methods developed for novel view synthesis evaluation transfer poorly to the forensic localization task. Ablation experiments indicate that both feature representations and correspondence quality contribute to localization performance, and code and dataset are available on GitHub.

## [Agentic Limit Order Books Show Phase Transitions and Non-Classical Market Impact](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31260) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.31260) studies limit order books populated exclusively by autonomous reinforcement-learning agents, identifying phase transitions between orderly price discovery and hyper-volatile cascade states governed by critical thresholds in agent count and market depth. It further shows that market impact under agentic liquidity provision deviates from the classical square-root law, exhibiting dissipative, balanced, and non-dissipative regimes under non-linear feedback loops. As AI-driven trading agents proliferate, understanding how purely agentic markets behave at the macroscopic level becomes critical for regulators, exchanges, and market participants. The finding that agentic liquidity can break the widely observed square-root market impact law suggests that existing execution models and risk assumptions may need revision in AI-dominated markets. The study formalizes agent interactions within a microscopic order-matching engine and identifies distinct phase boundaries separating orderly price discovery from hyper-volatile cascades. The deviation from square-root impact is characterized across dissipative, balanced, and non-dissipative regimes, driven by non-linear feedback loops among learning agents.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: A limit order book (LOB) is the electronic record of buy and sell limit orders used by most exchanges, with a matching engine determining which orders execute. Multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) studies multiple learning agents that share an environment and pursue their own rewards, producing complex group dynamics. The square-root law of market impact is a well-established empirical regularity stating that a meta-order's price change scales roughly with the square root of its volume, and it underpins much of modern execution and risk management.

## [MoSAR Learns Adaptive Attention Geometry for Long-Context Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31261) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.31261) introduces Mixture of Semantic Attention Regimes (MoSAR), which treats attention approximation as a geometric problem and learns an input-dependent, controlled-decay geometry over query-key interactions. Using post-RoPE bilinear query and key routers, MoSAR selects mixtures over short, medium, and global regimes, and in matched 500M-parameter pre-training experiments it improves perplexity over dense RoPE at the training context length while achieving the best perplexity under length extrapolation, including against ALiBi. Long-context language modeling is bottlenecked by the quadratic cost of dense self-attention, and MoSAR suggests that instead of hard-coding sparsity patterns, models can learn where positional relevance should decay. If the learned geometry remains stable under top-1 discretization, it could enable cheaper inference for long-context transformers, an area of intense interest across the LLM ecosystem. MoSAR induces a continuous distance-dependent attention field rather than a fixed sparsity pattern, and the learned geometry can be discretized through deterministic top-1 routing at inference time. The controlled experiments use matched 500M-parameter models, and the paper reports lower-reach attention geometry without degrading language-modeling quality.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Transformer self-attention compares every token with every other token, so its compute and memory grow quadratically with sequence length, making long-context modeling expensive. Efficient attention methods typically predefine sparsity or locality patterns, such as sliding windows or ALiBi's fixed distance bias, but these patterns may not match the input-dependent dependencies of natural language. Rotary Position Embedding (RoPE) is a widely used positional encoding that injects relative position information into query-key interactions, and MoSAR builds its routers on top of it.

## [Softmax Reparameterization Boosts Output-Head Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31291) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.31291) proposes softmax reparameterization, a post-training method that selects a functionally equivalent output head before quantization by subtracting a scalar multiple of the vocabulary-row mean from every output row. The coefficient is chosen via a one-dimensional validation-KL search for RTN, activation-weighted MSE, and full-Hessian GPTQ, and on Phi-4-mini it cuts AW-MSE KL divergence from 0.936 to 0.256. Large vocabularies make output heads a major inference cost in small language models, so a post-training trick that improves W4 fidelity without changing the trained decoder or adding inference operations is directly useful for efficient LLM deployment. The reported 10.8% batch-one latency reduction on Phi-4-mini shows the gains translate into real speedups rather than just better metrics. The one-dimensional search includes both the original head and fixed mean-centering as special cases, preserves the full-precision softmax distribution, and a rank-one correction handles nonlinear logit paths such as soft-capping. Frozen WikiText-selected coefficients transfer to C4 and OpenWebMath, outperforming mean-centering in all 18 comparisons where the coefficient differs from 1, and at W2 the benefits broaden across nearly the full model-quantizer matrix.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Post-training quantization compresses a trained model's weights to low bit-widths (e.g., W4 or W2) after training, with methods like RTN (round-to-nearest), GPTQ (which uses Hessian information to correct rounding error), and activation-weighted MSE minimizing quantization error. The output head maps hidden states to vocabulary logits before the softmax, so its size scales with vocabulary size and it can dominate inference cost in small models. Reparameterization here means rewriting the head in an algebraically equivalent form that is easier to quantize, without altering the model's full-precision predictions.

## [EffectMatch Validates Persistent Agent Outcomes at Runtime](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31301) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.31301) introduces EffectMatch, a runtime that collects persistent changes made within a controlled execution boundary and compares them against what the application approved for the current state and execution, using that comparison to govern commit and dependent execution. In comparative evaluation on 206 public business tasks, EffectMatch preserved all clean executions and prevented all tested incorrect commits, with six 20-run ablations and 80 task-topology cases confirming that each removed mechanism caused failures. LLM agents increasingly mutate databases and online services, yet current safeguards only approve an action or record its aftermath, so an unapproved persistent outcome can be silently accepted as success and propagated to later steps. EffectMatch addresses this gap by blocking silent acceptance and downstream propagation of persistent outcomes inconsistent with application approval, which matters for agent reliability and security in production software systems. The approach relies on a controlled execution boundary that captures persistent changes and compares them with approved effects for the current state and execution, rather than merely logging an action's aftermath. Evaluation includes 206 public business tasks, six 20-run ablations that expose the failure caused by each removed mechanism, and 80 task-topology cases that preserved truthful handoffs while blocking invalid continuation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: LLM agents are no longer limited to generating text; they can act on software systems by changing databases and online services. A single approved action, such as a database update, can produce additional persistent side effects, like an unapproved notification, because execution may go beyond the requested change. Existing safeguards can approve an action or record what happened afterward, but they do not check the persistent result before allowing execution to continue, so an unapproved outcome may be mistaken for success and propagated to later steps.

## [AgentXploit Automates Red-Teaming of AI Agents from Repo to Runtime](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31318) ⭐️ 7.0/10

AgentXploit introduces a two-role auditing system that separates repository-level attack-path discovery from runtime exploitation, with an Analyzer Agent tracing attacker-controlled inputs to sensitive operations and an Exploiter Agent converting those paths into concrete attacks refined via runtime feedback. The authors also release AgentXploit-Bench, containing 72 reproducible vulnerabilities across 12 open-source AI-agent systems, and report 59.3% end-to-end success versus 38.4% for Codex, plus 79.2% attack success on AgentDojo versus 52.7% for AgentVigil. This work addresses a critical security gap in agentic systems, where AI agents that modify files, call APIs, or execute code can be compromised by adversarial content or underlying software flaws such as path traversal and command injection. By automating authorized white-box pre-deployment auditing, it could help developers find and fix vulnerabilities before agents are deployed in production environments. The evaluation uses a controlled runtime where successful attacks must act through the task-defined attacker interface and be confirmed by an external verifier, and under a token-budget-matched comparison Codex reaches 46.3%. The separation of repository discovery and runtime exploitation is framed as two distinct challenges in end-to-end agent security auditing.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: AI agents combine language models with external data and tools, so security failures can arise when adversarial content changes an agent's tool use or when the surrounding software contains vulnerabilities such as path traversal (where user-supplied file names escape the intended directory) or command injection (where untrusted input is executed as system commands). White-box pre-deployment auditing means the auditor has access to the target repository and a controlled runtime, unlike black-box testing where only inputs and outputs are visible. AgentDojo is an existing benchmark for prompt-injection attacks on agents, and Codex and AgentVigil are prior systems used here as baselines.

## [DyMD Improves Interaction Dynamics in Few-Step Video World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31349) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose DyMD, a Distribution Matching Distillation framework that adapts teacher supervision and critic fitting to the evolving student, distilling a 14B teacher into a four-step 1.3B student with no auxiliary inference modules. On embodied-video benchmarks, the student improves R-Bench task adherence by 9.6 percentage points and PAI-Bench-G Domain score by 5.1 points over Base DMD, and achieves 34% mean success on two WorldArena planning tasks versus 16% for Base DMD. Few-step video diffusion is key to making large video world models practical for interactive embodied prediction and planning, where many-step sampling is too slow. By recovering robot-object motion that standard DMD suppresses, DyMD could make distilled world models more reliable as backbones for downstream action planning in robotics and embodied AI. The method combines temporal affinity-conditioned re-noise sampling, which mixes the base schedule with a teacher prior based on local posterior variation, and dynamics-guided fake-score tracking, which uses a noise-conditioned predictor to upweight hard rollouts in the critic loss. The result is a four-step 1.3B student distilled from a 14B teacher, with visual quality comparable to Base DMD.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Distribution Matching Distillation (DMD) is a technique that converts a slow multi-step diffusion model into a fast one-step or few-step generator by matching the synthetic and real data distributions, typically via a KL-divergence-style objective. Video diffusion models are increasingly used as 'world models' that predict future frames for embodied agents, but their many-step sampling is costly for interactive use. DyMD targets a known failure mode of DMD in this setting: it can preserve visual quality while suppressing the robot-object motion that embodied prediction depends on.

## [Safety-Bounded SDC-to-MCP Gateway for Medical AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31358) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.31358v1) presents an IEEE 11073 SDC-to-MCP gateway that exposes medical device metrics, alarms, context references, and semantic metadata as read-only resources, while representing selected action affordances as policy-validated dry-run tools. The gateway enforces a narrow no-execution property: agent-facing requests dispatch no SDC device operation, and a Python prototype validates this boundary across resource, proposal, and authorization paths. This work addresses a critical gap in safe agent-tool interaction for healthcare, where exposing device state and action affordances to language-model agents requires deterministic constraints on possible effects. It could inform future standards and implementations for AI safety in medical environments, affecting researchers and practitioners in AI safety, medical AI, and protocol design. The Python prototype supports simulated fault and lifecycle experiments, a software-reference protocol path spanning independent Java and Python implementations, deterministic baselines, representation ablations, and multi-model agent evaluation. Results show semantically explicit resource exposure, visible rejection of invalid or outdated state, and preservation of the no-execution boundary; explicit semantic metadata improved conformity to required metric identifiers in structured alarm outputs, though retained structured-output failures reveal a distinction between plausible narrative answers and task-compliant machine-readable results.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: IEEE 11073 Service-Oriented Device Connectivity (SDC) is a family of standards defining a communication protocol for point-of-care medical devices, enabling manufacturer-independent device-to-device and device-to-IT-system interoperability based on a service-oriented architecture. The Model Context Protocol (MCP) is an open standard that lets AI applications discover and use external resources and tools, allowing language-model agents to ground reasoning in current system state. In medical settings, connecting these two worlds raises safety concerns because agents could otherwise trigger real device actions, motivating gateways that constrain effects.

## [H2S: Compress Long Contexts by Highlighting and Summarizing Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31382) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper proposes Highlight-Then-Summarize (H2S), a compress-then-reason paradigm that first identifies question-relevant, source-grounded evidence and then integrates it into a compact, question-conditioned summary before generating the final answer. It introduces the H2S-Dataset (6,647 examples from 11 benchmark families, averaging 43.9K tokens) and H2S-RL, which adds process-level rewards for evidence selection and summary construction, with H2S-14B scoring 32.60 on the seven-task H2S-Bench and outperforming Qwen3.8-27B by 10.17 points. Long-context understanding is a major bottleneck for LLMs because task-relevant evidence is sparse and scattered among large amounts of irrelevant content, and H2S shows that explicitly selecting and integrating evidence can improve reasoning while enabling much more compact generation. This matters for anyone deploying LLMs on long documents, conversations, or code, since it points toward cheaper inference and better accuracy under tight output budgets. Under a shared 128K input and 4K output budget, H2S-14B retains 97.1% of its 16K-budget performance with only a 4K output budget, and removing its summary lowers average performance by 8.47 points, while extending the output budget from 4K to 16K yields only 0.96 additional points. The paper also reports that H2S-14B achieves the highest Evidence-Summary Quality score, though the abstract is truncated and full methodological and comparison details are limited.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Large language models are increasingly asked to reason over lengthy inputs such as documents, conversations, and code, but their context windows and inference budgets are finite, and relevant information is often buried in redundant text. Prior work on long-context understanding includes retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) with contextual compression and filters, as well as long-context benchmarks like LongBench v2 and ∞Bench that stress-test models on very long inputs. Reinforcement learning with process-level rewards is a related technique that assigns credit to intermediate steps rather than only the final answer, which is what H2S-RL applies to evidence selection and summary construction.

## [ActKV: Action-Guided KV Cache Compression for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31395) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced ActKV, the first KV cache compression framework designed specifically for agentic LLM inference, which prioritizes KV entries critical to action generation rather than overall output quality. On long-trace tasks, ActKV retains 98.53% of FullKV's accuracy while using only 25.98% of peak KV cache memory, and achieves 3.97x token throughput and 3.58x task throughput over FullKV. Agentic LLM inference accumulates long KV caches across iterative observation-reasoning-action loops, creating severe memory overhead and throughput bottlenecks in serving systems. ActKV's action-centric compression could substantially improve memory efficiency and serving throughput for agentic AI deployments, making it immediately relevant to researchers and practitioners optimizing long-context agent workloads. ActKV combines three components: action-oriented KV cache eviction that exploits stable action access patterns, confidence-driven adaptive budget allocation using the LLM's intrinsic confidence, and page-aware compression management with three standardized primitives and customized kernels for practical throughput gains. The framework addresses challenges of iterative execution, dynamic memory demands, and scattered action-critical entries that complicate eviction policies and paged memory integration.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: In transformer-based LLMs, the KV cache stores key and value tensors from previous tokens so the model does not recompute them at each decoding step, but it grows linearly with context length and consumes large amounts of GPU memory. Agentic inference differs from single-turn generation because a single query becomes a multi-turn loop of decoding, tool calls, and observations, so the cache keeps growing across iterations. Existing compression methods such as eviction or quantization typically optimize overall output quality, while ActKV argues that in agent settings the quality of generated actions matters more than general text fidelity. PagedAttention, used in systems like vLLM, splits the KV cache into fixed-size pages to reduce memory fragmentation, and ActKV builds page-aware compression on top of this idea.

## [VLMs Read Sharp Contour Text but Miss Soft Shading Text in Multi-Layer Typography](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31403) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces DecoyBench, a 300-image dataset built with the Decoy Font method, where each image overlays sharp contour text on soft shading text. Evaluating six recent closed-source VLMs across three model families under two prompting conditions and two resolutions, the study found that models read contour text with near-human accuracy at high resolution but almost never fully extracted the shading text, while humans read both layers accurately. This work exposes a consistent behavioral limitation in VLMs when processing typographic structures with multiple spatial frequency layers, which has direct implications for OCR robustness and security against typographic attacks. As multimodal models are increasingly deployed in document understanding and content moderation, such blind spots could be exploited to hide malicious or misleading text from automated systems. The evaluation covered six recent closed-source models from three different model families, tested under naive and guided prompting at 512×512 and 64×64 resolutions. At low resolution, neither models nor humans could read the contour text, but the shading text was extracted with high accuracy, suggesting the limitation is tied to spatial frequency processing rather than a simple failure to detect text.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Vision-language models (VLMs) combine visual encoders with large language models to perform tasks such as optical character recognition (OCR) and image question answering. Typographic attacks exploit the fact that these models can be confused by text rendered in unusual styles or overlaid on images, and prior work has shown that such attacks can disrupt models like CLIP. The Decoy Font method creates text that appears as one message up close and another from a distance by combining a sharp contour layer with a soft shading layer, making it a useful probe for testing how VLMs handle multiple text layers.

## [Active Provenance Gate Blocks Fabricated Consensus in Multi-Agent Debate](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31422) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.31422) introduces the Active Provenance Gate (APG), a post-debate verification layer that audits each claim in a multi-agent debate (MAD) summary against the debate logs and blocks unsupported claims before publication. In crisis simulations, its self-healing mechanism more than doubled average data Provenance Fidelity in difficult scenarios, and over 75% of human study participants preferred a report that explicitly stated failure over a fluent but fabricated consensus. Multi-agent debate is increasingly used as a decision pipeline in distributed and high-stakes processes, yet the final synthesis step has remained largely uncontrolled, allowing summarizing models to invent consensus that the debate never reached. By shifting provenance from passive logging to active conditional blocking, APG addresses a genuine safety gap that could affect any organization relying on LLM debate outputs for critical decisions. The APG treats the debate log as a hard constraint, auditing each claim and applying self-correction before a strict gate blocks unsupported claims and generates divergence reports when no reliable compromise exists. Notably, most human participants perceived the fabricated consensus from the baseline system as more fluent, suggesting that fluency is a poor proxy for faithfulness in synthesis.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 28, 04:00

**Background**: Multi-agent debate (MAD) systems have LLM agents argue different positions and then use a summarizing model to produce a final consensus, which improves reasoning and can let small models match larger ones. However, LLMs are prone to hallucination—generating fluent text unsupported by their inputs—and in MAD the synthesis phase is especially vulnerable because the summarizer may smooth over disagreements. Provenance verification, which traces claims back to their sources, is an emerging approach to detecting such unsupported content.
